The invariant metrics of L.1 and L.2 have the stated common value at zero, so [Symmetric spaces B.2](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-b-2) identifies their Levi-Civita connections with the canonical ones and gives \(R=-[[\,\cdot,\cdot\,],\cdot]\). Applying this to (L.19) proves (L.16), including its sign.

This also realizes the algebraic duality of D.3 explicitly. In the complexified compact algebra, replace its odd space by \(i\mathfrak m\); conjugation by \(\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-iI_q)\) fixes the even space and takes \(iX_+(Z)\) to \(X_-(Z)\). Restricting to total trace zero gives the corresponding pair of special-unitary algebras, with the same odd brackets. The scalar even centre acts trivially on the quotient and has no effect on these tangent computations.

Putting \(W=iZ\) and \(V=iZ\) in (L.16) gives \(R_+(Z,iZ)iZ=4ZZ^*Z\). Also \(Z\) and \(iZ\) are real-orthogonal and have equal norm, so the denominator defining sectional curvature is exactly the one in (L.17). This proves that formula. If the nonzero eigenvalues of \(Z^*Z\) are \(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r>0\), then \(r\leq m\) by K.1 and
\[
\frac1m\leq\frac1r
\leq\frac{\sum_j\lambda_j^2}{(\sum_j\lambda_j)^2}\leq1.
\]
The upper bound follows by expanding the squared sum. The lower bound follows from
\(r\sum_j\lambda_j^2-(\sum_j\lambda_j)^2=\sum_{j<k}(\lambda_j-\lambda_k)^2\geq0\).
Rank-one matrices attain the upper ratio, and rectangular diagonal matrices with \(m\) equal positive singular values attain the lower ratio. Thus the claimed bounds have their stated exact constants. □

**Example L.4 (The projective line and the disc).** For \(p=q=1\), the compact chart and the noncompact domain have metrics
\[
g_+=\frac{|dz|^2}{(1+|z|^2)^2},
\qquad
g_-=\frac{|dz|^2}{(1-|z|^2)^2},
\tag{L.20}
\]
of constant Gaussian curvature \(4\) and \(-4\), respectively. The Cayley map
\[
w=i\,\frac{1+z}{1-z}
\tag{L.21}
\]
maps the unit disc biholomorphically onto the upper half-plane and pulls its metric \(|dw|^2/(\operatorname{Im}w)^2\) back to \(4g_-\), of curvature \(-1\).

**Proof.** Formula (L.20) is (L.3) and (L.12) for scalar matrices. In complex dimension one the real tangent plane is the plane spanned by \(Z,iZ\), so its Gaussian curvature is the holomorphic sectional curvature in (L.17), namely \(4\) or \(-4\). Compactness is that of the full projective line, not of its single graph chart.

For (L.21), direct subtraction of its conjugate gives
\[
\operatorname{Im}w=\frac{1-|z|^2}{|1-z|^2},
\qquad
dw=\frac{2i\,dz}{(1-z)^2}.
\tag{L.22}
\]
Its inverse is \(z=(w-i)/(w+i)\); positivity of the imaginary part makes \(|w-i|<|w+i|\), proving that it lands in the disc. Both denominators are nonzero on their respective domains, so the two rational maps are inverse holomorphic maps. Substituting (L.22) proves the metric factor four. Multiplying a Riemannian metric by a positive constant leaves its Levi-Civita connection unchanged by the Koszul formula in [Symmetric spaces A.1](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-a-1). Its sectional-curvature numerator scales by that constant and its denominator by its square; therefore curvature is divided by the constant. This gives curvature \(-1\) for \(4g_-\). □

## M. The Siegel upper half-space and its bounded coordinates

For an integer \(r\geq1\), let
\[
\mathcal H_r=\{W=X+iY: X,Y\in M_r(\mathbb R),\ X^T=X,\ Y^T=Y,\ Y>0\}.
\tag{M.1}
\]
It is an open convex subset of the complex vector space of symmetric matrices, of complex dimension \(r(r+1)/2\). Positivity is open by K.1; it is convex because each quadratic form remains positive under convex combinations. Its tangent vectors are complex symmetric matrices.

**Theorem M.1 (The symplectic action and the full stabilizer).** With
\[
\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_r\\-I_r&0\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb R)=\{g\in GL(2r,\mathbb R):g^T\Omega g=\Omega\},
\tag{M.2}
\]
the formula
\[
g\cdot W=(AW+B)(CW+D)^{-1},
\qquad
g=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&D\end{pmatrix},
\tag{M.3}
\]
is a transitive holomorphic action on \(\mathcal H_r\). The group is connected, and the stabilizer of \(iI_r\) is
\[
K=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\-B&A\end{pmatrix}: A-iB\in U(r)\right\}
\cong U(r).
\tag{M.4}
\]
The resulting identification \(\mathrm{Sp}(2r,\mathbb R)/K\cong\mathcal H_r\) is a diffeomorphism.

**Proof.** Write \(F_W=\binom{W}{I_r}\). Since \(W^T=W\) and \(Y>0\),
\[
F_W^T\Omega F_W=0,\qquad F_W^*\Omega F_W=-2iY.
\tag{M.5}
\]
If \(N=CW+D\) had a nonzero kernel vector \(v\), then \(gF_Wv\) would have lower block zero and hence have zero pairing with itself under \(\Omega\). But the second identity in (M.5), and reality of \(g\), make that pairing \(-2i\,v^*Yv\ne0\). Thus \(N\) is invertible.

Set \(W'=(AW+B)N^{-1}\). The graph equation
\[
gF_W=F_{W'}N
\tag{M.6}
\]
and the first identity in (M.5) imply \(W'^T=W'\). The second identity then gives
\[
Y'=\operatorname{Im}W'=N^{-*}YN^{-1}>0.
\tag{M.7}
\]
So (M.3) maps the domain into itself. Equation (M.6), applied twice, proves the action law because matrix multiplication is associative and the normalized graph has unique upper block. The identity acts trivially; the inverse matrix gives the inverse action. Its entries are holomorphic rational functions with nonvanishing denominator, proving holomorphicity.

For any \(W=X+iY\), let \(S=Y^{1/2}\), the real positive square root supplied by K.1, and put
\[
g_W=\begin{pmatrix}S&XS^{-1}\\0&S^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{M.8}
\]
Multiplying the blocks shows \(g_W^T\Omega g_W=\Omega\): the upper-right relation is \(S^TS^{-1}=I\), the lower-right skew block vanishes because \(X=X^T\), and the other relations are their transposes. Its action sends \(iI_r\) to \(X+iY\). This proves transitivity and gives a smooth global section.

Fixing \(iI_r\) in (M.3) means \(iA+B=-C+iD\), hence \(D=A\), \(C=-B\). Substitution in the symplectic equations gives
\[
A^TA+B^TB=I,\qquad A^TB=B^TA.
\tag{M.9}
\]
These are exactly \((A-iB)^*(A-iB)=I\). The real block matrix in (M.4) is the real representation of the complex matrix \(A-iB\), so this identification preserves multiplication and gives the full stabilizer, not only its identity component.

The symplectic group is a closed matrix Lie subgroup by [Invariant connections A.1](invariant-connections-on-homogeneous-bundles.md#theorem-a-1). It is connected: the segment
\(W_t=tX+i((1-t)I+tY)\) stays in \(\mathcal H_r\), and (M.8) supplies a path from the identity to \(g_W\). Every \(g\) equals \(g_{g\cdot iI}k\) with \(k\in K\), and K.1 makes \(K\) connected. Finally [Local tools 4.1](local-tools-for-bundles-and-transport.md#4-quotients-by-a-closed-lie-subgroup) gives the quotient manifold. The orbit map descends smoothly to the quotient; its inverse is \(W\mapsto g_WK\), smooth by (M.8). They are inverse by transitivity and the stabilizer calculation. □

**Theorem M.2 (The metric and global point symmetries).** The Hermitian form
\[
\begin{aligned}
h_W(U,V)&=\operatorname{tr}(Y^{-1}\bar UY^{-1}V),\\
g_W(U,V)&=\operatorname{Re}h_W(U,V),
\qquad U^T=U,\ V^T=V,
\end{aligned}
\tag{M.10}
\]
is positive and invariant under (M.3). It is Kähler and makes \(\mathcal H_r\) a complete simply connected Hermitian symmetric space. Its point symmetry at \(iI_r\) is \(W\mapsto-W^{-1}\). At \(W_0=X_0+iY_0\) its point symmetry is
\[
s_{W_0}(W)=X_0-Y_0(W-X_0)^{-1}Y_0.
\tag{M.11}
\]

**Proof.** Because \(U\) is symmetric, \(U^*=\bar U\). Hence
\[
h_W(U,U)=\|Y^{-1/2}UY^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2>0
\quad(U\ne0).
\tag{M.12}
\]
Conjugate symmetry follows from taking the conjugate transpose inside the trace, and complex linearity in the second variable and conjugate linearity in the first are immediate. Thus \(g\) is a smooth positive Hermitian metric with exactly the scale in (M.10).

Let \(N=CW+D\) as in M.1. Differentiation of (M.3) gives
\[
d(g\cdot)_W(U)=(A-W'C)UN^{-1}=N^{-T}UN^{-1}.
\tag{M.13}
\]
For the last equality, multiply \(A-W'C\) on the left by \(N^T\). Symmetry of \(W'\) and \(W'N=AW+B\) give
\[
N^T(A-W'C)
=(WC^T+D^T)A-(WA^T+B^T)C=I.
\]
Here \(C^TA=A^TC\) and \(D^TA-B^TC=I\) are the corresponding blocks of \(g^T\Omega g=\Omega\).

The real symmetric matrix \(Y'\) in (M.7) gives two equal expressions for its inverse:
\[
Y'^{-1}=NY^{-1}\bar N^T
       =\bar NY^{-1}N^T.
\tag{M.14}
\]
Use the first expression before \(\overline{d(g\cdot)U}\), and the second before \(d(g\cdot)V\). Equations (M.13)–(M.14) yield
\[
\begin{aligned}
&Y'^{-1}\overline{d(g\cdot)U}\,Y'^{-1}d(g\cdot)V\\
&\quad=
(NY^{-1}\bar N^T)(\bar N^{-T}\bar U\bar N^{-1})
(\bar NY^{-1}N^T)(N^{-T}VN^{-1})\\
&\quad=NY^{-1}\bar UY^{-1}VN^{-1}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{M.15}
\]
Taking traces proves invariance, without interchanging noncommuting matrix factors.

The symplectic matrix \(\begin{pmatrix}0&-I\\I&0\end{pmatrix}\) acts by \(s(W)=-W^{-1}\). The inverse exists: if \(Wv=0\), then \(\operatorname{Im}(v^*Wv)=v^*Yv>0\) for \(v\ne0\), a contradiction. This holomorphic isometry fixes \(iI\); its differential is \(U\mapsto W^{-1}UW^{-1}\), equal to \(-U\) at \(iI\). Conjugate it by the transitive action in M.1 to obtain point symmetries everywhere.

More explicitly the map associated to (M.8) for \(W_0\) is
\(F(Q)=X_0+S_0QS_0\), with \(S_0=Y_0^{1/2}\). Its inverse is
\(F^{-1}(W)=S_0^{-1}(W-X_0)S_0^{-1}\). Substituting into \(F\circ s\circ F^{-1}\) proves (M.11). The inverse of \(W-X_0\) exists by the same imaginary-part argument, so this formula is global.

Theorem A.2 makes a Hermitian metric with these holomorphic point symmetries Kähler. [Symmetric spaces A.2](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-a-2) proves its geodesic completeness. Convexity from (M.1) contracts every loop by the straight-line homotopy to \(iI\); thus the space is simply connected. None of these conclusions requires an external integrability or completeness theorem. □

**Proposition M.3 (The symmetric matrix ball and its factor four).** The transformations
\[
Z=(W-iI)(W+iI)^{-1},\qquad
W=i(I+Z)(I-Z)^{-1}
\tag{M.16}
\]
are inverse biholomorphisms between \(\mathcal H_r\) and
\[
\mathcal B_r=\{Z\in M_r(\mathbb C):Z^T=Z,\ I-Z^*Z>0\}.
\tag{M.17}
\]
Under these coordinates the metric (M.10) is
\[
h^{\mathcal B}_Z(U,V)
=4\operatorname{tr}\bigl((I-Z^*Z)^{-1}U^*(I-ZZ^*)^{-1}V\bigr),
\quad U^T=U,\quad V^T=V.
\tag{M.18}
\]
The symmetry at the origin is \(Z\mapsto-Z\). For \(r=1\) this is the curvature-\(-1\) metric of L.4.

**Proof.** For \(W\in\mathcal H_r\), \(W+iI\) is invertible by positivity of its imaginary part, as in M.2. The resulting \(Z\) is symmetric: the two polynomials in \(W\) commute with each other and with their inverses, so transposition leaves their product unchanged. Moreover
\[
\begin{aligned}
&(W+iI)^*(I-Z^*Z)(W+iI)\\
&\qquad=(W+iI)^*(W+iI)-(W-iI)^*(W-iI)=4Y>0.
\end{aligned}
\tag{M.19}
\]
This proves membership in (M.17).

Conversely, if \(Z\in\mathcal B_r\), then \(I-Z\) is invertible: \(Zv=v\ne0\) would contradict \(v^*(I-Z^*Z)v>0\). Set \(A=I-Z\), \(P=I-Z^*Z\), \(Q=I-ZZ^*\). Transposition again proves that the inverse formula in (M.16) is symmetric. Direct subtraction of its conjugate transpose gives
\[
Y=A^{-*}PA^{-1}>0.
\tag{M.20}
\]
Indeed, after multiplication by \(A^*\) and \(A\), the sum
\(A^*(I+Z)+(I+Z^*)A\) is \(2P\). Since \(W\) is symmetric, its imaginary part is real symmetric, so this is a point of \(\mathcal H_r\). Solving the two elementary linear-fractional equations proves that the maps in (M.16) are inverse. Their rational entries are holomorphic on the domains just verified.

For completeness compute the metric, not only the complex structure. Differentiating the inverse map gives
\[
dW(U)=2iA^{-1}UA^{-1}.
\tag{M.21}
\]
To obtain this, differentiate \(i(I+Z)A^{-1}\), then use
\(I+(I+Z)A^{-1}=2A^{-1}\). Since \(Z\) is symmetric, \(A^*=\bar A\), \(P^T=Q\); (M.20) and reality of \(Y\) imply
\[
Y^{-1}=AP^{-1}\bar A=\bar A Q^{-1}A.
\tag{M.22}
\]
Insert (M.21) and its conjugate in (M.10), using the first expression in (M.22) before the conjugate tangent and the second before the other tangent. The product inside the trace reduces to
\[
4AP^{-1}\bar UQ^{-1}VA^{-1}.
\]
Since \(U^*=\bar U\), cyclicity gives (M.18). Substitution of \(-W^{-1}\) into the first map of (M.16) gives \(-Z\), or equivalently substitution of \(-Z\) into its inverse gives \(-W^{-1}\). At zero (M.18) is \(4\operatorname{tr}(U^*V)\), explaining its factor four relative to the matrix-ball metric (L.12) restricted to symmetric tangents. Its scalar case and curvature are exactly those of L.4, with the map and metric calculation (L.21)–(L.22). □

## N. A fully normalized Siegel exercise

**Exercise N.1 (Action, metric and reflection).** Verify the Hermitian symmetric structure on \(\mathcal H_r\), including the domain of the action, its stabilizer, the scale of the metric, and a global point symmetry. State the resulting disc metric when \(r=1\).

**Solution.** Use the alternating form \(\Omega\) and graph column \(F_W\) from (M.2) and (M.5). Their identities prove both invertibility of \(CW+D\) and positivity of
\[
\operatorname{Im}(g\cdot W)=(CW+D)^{-*}Y(CW+D)^{-1}.
\]
The graph multiplication (M.6) proves the action law, and the section (M.8) sends \(iI\) to every \(X+iY\). Solving the stabilizer equations gives \(D=A,\ C=-B\) and \(A-iB\in U(r)\), so it is the full group in (M.4).

At \(iI\) choose exactly
\[
h_{iI}(U,V)=\operatorname{tr}(\bar U V).
\]
The derivative and the two inverse-imaginary-part formulas (M.13)–(M.15) prove that its invariant extension is (M.10), with no additional scalar. In particular, for symmetric \(U\),
\[
h_{iI}(U,U)=\sum_a|U_{aa}|^2+2\sum_{a<b}|U_{ab}|^2.
\tag{N.1}
\]
The factor two on off-diagonal symmetric coordinates comes from counting both entries in the trace; replacing this by the unweighted norm of the independent upper-triangular entries would give a different form.

The matrix \(\begin{pmatrix}0&-I\\I&0\end{pmatrix}\) gives the symmetry \(W\mapsto-W^{-1}\) at \(iI\), with derivative \(-I\); (M.11) gives the global symmetry at \(X_0+iY_0\). Invariance makes these maps isometries, and rationality makes them holomorphic. A.2 and [Symmetric spaces A.2](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-a-2) supply the Kähler condition and completeness. Convexity contracts loops, proving simple connectivity.

Finally the Cayley transform has differential \(dW=2i\,dZ\) at zero. Thus the upper-half-space metric corresponds at zero to \(4\operatorname{tr}(U^*V)\) on the bounded model; M.3 proves the global formula (M.18). For \(r=1\) this is
\[
\frac{4|dz|^2}{(1-|z|^2)^2}
\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
\frac{|dw|^2}{(\operatorname{Im}w)^2},
\]
both of curvature \(-1\) by L.4. This verifies the action, geometry and every stated normalization. □

## O. Positive Cartan matrices

We next classify a finite matrix object before relating it to roots. A **positive symmetrizable Cartan matrix** is an integral \(n\times n\) matrix \(A\), \(n\geq1\), such that
\[
a_{ii}=2,\qquad a_{ij}\leq0\ (i\ne j),\qquad
a_{ij}=0\Longleftrightarrow a_{ji}=0,
\tag{O.1}
\]
and a positive diagonal matrix \(D=\operatorname{diag}(d_i)\) makes \(DA\) symmetric positive definite. Its graph joins \(i,j\) when \(a_{ij}\ne0\). Connectedness is equivalent to indecomposability under simultaneous row and column permutation: a partition with no crossing edges is exactly a diagonal block decomposition.

Our convention in this part and the next is
\[
a_{ij}=\frac{2(\alpha_i,\alpha_j)}{(\alpha_i,\alpha_i)}.
\tag{O.2}
\]
The denominator belongs to the row. This transposes the column convention in [De Rham decomposition AE.1](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#lemma-ae-1). We retain the geometric arrow convention: a multiple edge points from a longer vector to a shorter one.

**Lemma O.1 (normalization and length data).** Put \(k_{ij}=a_{ij}a_{ji}\) for distinct vertices. Then \(k_{ij}\in\{0,1,2,3\}\), and
\[
Q=D^{-1/2}(DA)D^{-1/2},\qquad
Q_{ii}=2,\quad Q_{ij}=-\sqrt{k_{ij}}
\tag{O.3}
\]
is positive definite. There are independent vectors \(\alpha_i\) with Gram matrix \(DA\); they satisfy (O.2) and \(\|\alpha_i\|^2=2d_i\). Along an edge,
\[
\frac{d_j}{d_i}=\frac{a_{ij}}{a_{ji}}.
\tag{O.4}
\]
Consequently the symmetrizer is unique up to multiplication by a positive constant on each connected component. An edge has multiplicity \(k_{ij}\); together with the arrow toward smaller \(d_i\), this determines its two Cartan entries.

**Proof.** Symmetry gives \(d_i a_{ij}=d_j a_{ji}\). Congruence by the invertible diagonal matrix \(D^{-1/2}\) preserves positivity, since its quadratic form at \(x\ne0\) is that of \(DA\) at \(D^{-1/2}x\ne0\). For an edge both entries are negative, so its off-diagonal entry is
\[
\sqrt{d_i/d_j}\,a_{ij}=-\sqrt{a_{ij}a_{ji}}.
\]
Restriction to the two coordinate directions has quadratic form
\[
2x^2-2\sqrt{k_{ij}}xy+2y^2
=2\bigl(x-\tfrac12\sqrt{k_{ij}}y\bigr)^2
+\bigl(2-\tfrac12k_{ij}\bigr)y^2.
\]
Thus \(k_{ij}<4\). It is a nonnegative integer, proving the list.

Give \(\mathbb R^n\) the inner product \(x^tDAy\), and take its coordinate basis for \(\alpha_i\). This constructs the vectors directly and verifies (O.2). Equation (O.4) propagates the ratios of all \(d_i\) along paths. It proves the uniqueness assertion, including singleton components.

On a single edge the entries are \((-1,-1)\). On a double or triple edge their absolute values have product two or three, so one is one and the other is respectively two or three. Equation (O.4) puts the larger absolute entry in the row of the shorter vector. Thus for vertices ordered long then short the entries are \((-1,-2)\) or \((-1,-3)\). This also proves that neither the multiplicities nor the arrows depend on the chosen symmetrizer. □

**Theorem O.2 (the complete positive Cartan list).** Up to simultaneous permutation of rows and columns, the connected matrices of (O.1) are exactly the following:

| Type | Graph and row convention |
|---|---|
| \(A_n,\ n\geq1\) | A chain of \(n\) vertices, all edges single |
| \(B_n,\ n\geq2\) | The chain \(1,\ldots,n\), last edge double, with \((a_{n-1,n},a_{n,n-1})=(-1,-2)\) |
| \(C_n,\ n\geq2\) | The same chain, with last pair \((-2,-1)\) |
| \(D_n,\ n\geq4\) | One branch, with the single-edge arm triple \((1,1,n-3)\) |
| \(E_6,E_7,E_8\) | One branch, with single-edge arm triples \((1,2,2)\), \((1,2,3)\), \((1,2,4)\), respectively |
| \(F_4\) | The chain \(1,2,3,4\), middle edge double, with \((a_{23},a_{32})=(-1,-2)\) |
| \(G_2\) | Two vertices, with \((a_{12},a_{21})=(-1,-3)\) |

All diagonal entries are two, both entries on a single edge are minus one, and entries outside the edges are zero. Arm lengths count edges from the branch. The two names \(B_2,C_2\) describe the same matrix up to exchanging vertices; for \(n\geq3\) the \(B_n,C_n\) types are distinct. Reversing a path identifies the two arrow orientations in each of \(F_4,G_2\). Apart from the stated rank-two duplication, the table has no repetitions.

**Proof.** By O.1, \(Q\) is twice the Gram matrix of independent unit vectors, with off-diagonal entries \(-\sqrt{k}/2\) in that Gram matrix. The restrictions proved in [De Rham decomposition AE.2](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#theorem-ae-2) use exactly these facts about the Gram matrix; they do not use reflection closure or any other root axiom. They therefore apply to the vectors just constructed, even though no root system has yet been specified. Here are the complete restrictions and their positivity tests.

The graph is a tree: coefficients one on a cycle with \(m\) vertices give quadratic value at most \(2m-2m=0\), with any extra edges decreasing it. On the star of a vertex, whose neighbours are pairwise unjoined, completing the neighbour squares gives central coefficient
\[
2-\frac12\sum_{j\sim i}k_{ij}>0.
\tag{O.5}
\]
Thus the sum is at most three. The degree is at most three; three incident edges must all be single; a triple edge fills the bound at both ends and is an isolated \(G_2\) component.

The three remaining forbidden configurations have the explicit nonpositive test vectors of [De Rham decomposition AE.2](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#theorem-ae-2), equations (AE.8)–(AE.10). We spell them out to make the matrix scope clear. Between two branch vertices choose a shortest path with \(m\) vertices, assign two to each path vertex and one to two further neighbours of each endpoint. Its quadratic value is at most
\[
(8m+8)-8(m-1)-16=0.
\]
The extra neighbours are distinct because the graph is a tree. Two double edges would give a path with double end edges and single intervening edges; adjacent double edges are already excluded by (O.5). Give its outer endpoints coefficient one and its interior vertices coefficient \(\sqrt2\). For \(m\) vertices the value is
\[
(4m-4)-8-4(m-3)=0.
\]
If a branch and a double edge coexist, follow a path from the branch through the first double edge to its far endpoint. Give the \(m\) vertices before that endpoint coefficient two, the far endpoint \(\sqrt2\), and two other neighbours of the branch coefficient one. The value is
\[
(8m+8)-8(m-1)-8-8=0.
\]
All contradict positivity. Hence there is at most one branch or one double edge, and they cannot coexist.

To bound the possible lengths, let \(T_l\) be the single-edge chain matrix with diagonal two. The calculations in the same earlier proof are
\[
\begin{aligned}
x^tT_lx&=x_1^2+x_l^2+\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}(x_i-x_{i+1})^2>0
       &&(x\ne0),\\
(T_l^{-1})_{11}&=\frac{l}{l+1}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{O.6}
\]
For \(l=1\), the first expression means \(2x_1^2\). To check the inverse entry, substitute \(z_i=(l+1-i)/(l+1)\) in \(T_lz=e_1\). Completing squares in a block matrix gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
d t^2+2t b^ty+y^tBy
={}&(y+B^{-1}bt)^tB(y+B^{-1}bt)\\
&+(d-b^tB^{-1}b)t^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{O.7}
\]
For three arms \(1\leq p\leq q\leq r\), eliminate their positive chain blocks with (O.6)–(O.7). The central coefficient is positive exactly when
\[
\frac1{p+1}+\frac1{q+1}+\frac1{r+1}>1.
\tag{O.8}
\]
If \(p\geq2\), the left side is at most one. Thus \(p=1\). When \(q=1\), every \(r\geq1\) works, giving \(D_{r+3}\). When \(q\geq3\), the sum is at most \(1/2+1/4+1/4\). The remaining case \(q=2\) requires \(r<5\), so \(r=2,3,4\), giving the three \(E\) types.

For a chain with one double edge, removing it gives blocks with \(p,q\geq1\) vertices. Eliminate the first block. The second form is
\[
y^tT_qy-\frac{2p}{p+1}y_1^2.
\]
For completeness put \(u=T_q^{-1}e_1\), \(a=u_1=q/(q+1)\), and \(y=tu+z\) with \(z_1=0\), \(t=y_1/a\). Since \(u^tT_qz=0\), this form becomes
\[
z^tT_qz+t^2a\left(1-\frac{2p}{p+1}a\right).
\]
It is positive definite exactly when
\[
1-\frac{2pq}{(p+1)(q+1)}>0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(p-1)(q-1)<2.
\tag{O.9}
\]
The possibilities are \(p=1\), \(q=1\), or \(p=q=2\). They give the end-double chains \(B,C\) or \(F_4\). A tree with no branch and no multiple edge is \(A_n\).

These tests also prove existence for every matrix in the table, without assuming a root realization. For a simply laced graph take all \(d_i=1\). For a multiple edge assign \(d_i=2\) on its long side and one on its short side, or three and one for \(G_2\); every other edge is single and propagates its endpoint value unchanged. Then \(DA\) is symmetric. Its normalized matrix is positive by (O.6), (O.8) or (O.9); for \(G_2\) the two-coordinate square completion in O.1 is positive with \(k=3\). Congruence gives positivity of \(DA\).

Finally the tree shape, arm lengths, location of the multiple edge and its orientation recover the table entries uniquely. The two ends of the rank-two chain can be exchanged. For longer end-double chains, the vertex at the double edge which is an endpoint is intrinsic: its being short or long distinguishes \(B_n\) from \(C_n\). In \(F_4\) and \(G_2\), path reversal exchanges the drawings. For branch types, the unordered arm triple gives exactly the ranks and types stated. This proves both exhaustion and the claimed identifications. □

## P. Roots, changes of simple basis and classification

A **reduced crystallographic root system** in a Euclidean space \(V\) is a finite spanning set \(R\) of nonzero vectors such that
\[
s_\alpha(x)=x-\frac{2(x,\alpha)}{(\alpha,\alpha)}\alpha
\tag{P.1}
\]
permutes \(R\) for every \(\alpha\in R\), every number \(2(\beta,\alpha)/(\alpha,\alpha)\) is integral, and the only roots on the line through \(\alpha\) are \(\alpha,-\alpha\). A **simple basis** is a basis \(\Delta=(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_n)\) in \(R\) such that every root has integer coefficients all nonnegative or all nonpositive in this basis. A vector is **regular** if its inner product with every root is nonzero. We use the row Cartan convention (O.2).

**Theorem P.1 (realization and uniqueness with a specified basis).** Every positive symmetrizable Cartan matrix, connected or not, is the row Cartan matrix of a reduced crystallographic root system with an ordered simple basis. For a specified symmetrizer \(D\), the realization can be chosen with Gram matrix \(DA\). Any two realizations with the same ordered Cartan matrix are related by the unique linear map taking one simple basis to the other; this map is a similarity on each connected component.

For every root system a simple basis exists. The sign-coefficient definition above is equivalent to being the indecomposable positive roots for some regular vector. If \(W\) is generated by the reflections in a simple basis, then
\[
R=\bigcup_{i=1}^n W\alpha_i.
\tag{P.2}
\]
The graph components give precisely the orthogonal irreducible components of \(R\).

**Proof.** [De Rham decomposition AE.1](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#lemma-ae-1) proves from the abstract root axioms that, for each regular vector, the positive roots which cannot be split into two positive roots form a basis, and that every positive root has a nonnegative integral expansion in it. Its proof explicitly supplies the root-difference argument for abstract systems, so this result needs no Lie-algebra origin. The existence of a regular vector is also proved there using the finite-hyperplane argument in [De Rham decomposition AD.5](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#lemma-ad-5).

Conversely, let \(\Delta\) be a basis with the sign-coefficient property. Solve \((v,\alpha_i)=1\) for \(v\): the Gram matrix is invertible because its quadratic form is the squared norm of a nonzero linear combination of a basis. Every root then pairs with \(v\) as a nonzero integer of its coefficient sign, so \(v\) is regular. A basis root cannot split into two positive roots, since its unit coefficient vector cannot be the sum of two nonzero nonnegative integer vectors. The indecomposable positive roots form a basis by the preceding programme result and already contain the \(n\) members of \(\Delta\). They therefore equal \(\Delta\). This proves the equivalence and makes the orbit and component conclusions of [De Rham decomposition AE.1](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#lemma-ae-1) applicable to every simple basis as defined here. In particular that proof's height descent gives (P.2).

Now take a matrix \(A\). Its connected blocks are on the list of O.2. Complete coordinate realizations with all root axioms and all simple-basis sign checks are proved in [De Rham decomposition AE.3](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#lemma-ae-3) for \(A,B,C,D\), AE.4 for \(E_6,E_7,E_8\), and AE.5 for \(F_4,G_2\). Transpose their recorded column Cartan matrices to obtain our row convention. The \(B,C\) coordinates then have exactly the end entries of O.2. The \(F_4\) basis has its first two roots long and its last two short, giving \(a_{23}=-1,a_{32}=-2\). For \(G_2\), exchange the earlier basis order, which was short then long, to obtain our long-first order and entries \(-1,-3\). Use the \(B_2\) realization with exchanged labels for \(C_2\). All further vertex permutations in O.2 are implemented by reordering the same basis.

Take the orthogonal direct sum of these Euclidean spaces, with root set the union of their root sets in their respective summands. It is finite, spans, and is reduced. A reflection in one summand acts identically on every other summand and permutes the roots in its own; cross-summand Cartan integers are zero. The union of the simple bases is a basis, and each root retains its one-sign expansion. Thus this is the required root system for every block matrix.

If \(d_i^0=\|\alpha_i\|^2/2\) in this realization, then \(\operatorname{diag}(d_i^0)A\) is its Gram matrix. O.1 implies that \(d_i/d_i^0=c>0\) is constant on each connected component. Multiply every vector in that component by \(\sqrt c\). Reflections and Cartan integers are unchanged, and the new Gram matrix is precisely \(DA\).

For uniqueness let \(T\alpha_i=\alpha_i'\) between two realizations with the same ordered matrix. The reflection formula is
\[
s_i\alpha_j=\alpha_j-a_{ij}\alpha_i.
\tag{P.3}
\]
It shows that \(T\) intertwines the corresponding simple reflections. Applying (P.2) gives \(T(R)=R'\). Along each graph edge the squared-length ratios are fixed by (O.4), so the two Gram matrices differ by a positive scalar on each connected component. Entries between components are zero. Thus \(T\) is a componentwise similarity.

For clarity the component assertion can also be read directly from (P.2): distinct component spans are orthogonal, each generating reflection fixes all other spans, and each root is in one component span. Those intersections are root systems. An orthogonal splitting of one of them would partition its simple roots into unjoined sets, contrary to connectedness. Conversely every orthogonal decomposition partitions the simple roots in just this way. This proves the asserted uniqueness of the irreducible components. □

**Theorem P.2 (independence of the simple basis).** Let \(\Delta,\Delta'\) be two simple bases of \(R\). The group \(W\) generated by the reflections in \(\Delta\) contains an element sending \(\Delta\) to \(\Delta'\) as unordered sets. Hence the two ordered Cartan matrices differ only by simultaneous row and column permutation, and their directed diagrams agree.

**Proof.** Every generator is an isometry permuting the finite spanning set \(R\). The map from \(W\) to its permutations is injective: a linear transformation fixing every root fixes their span. Thus \(W\) is finite.

Choose \(u,v\) by \((u,\alpha_i)=1\) for \(\Delta\) and \((v,\alpha_i')=1\) for \(\Delta'\). P.1 proves that these equations are solvable and that both vectors are regular, with positive systems determined by the two bases. Maximize \((wv,u)\) over \(w\in W\), possible by finiteness. If \((wv,\alpha_i)<0\), then
\[
(s_iwv,u)-(wv,u)
=-\frac{2(wv,\alpha_i)(u,\alpha_i)}{(\alpha_i,\alpha_i)}>0,
\]
contradicting the maximum. Equality \((wv,\alpha_i)=0\) is impossible: \(w^{-1}\alpha_i\) is a root and \(v\) is regular. Consequently \(wv\) pairs positively with every member of \(\Delta\).

The one-sign expansions imply that \(wv\) and \(u\) define exactly the same positive roots. Since \(w\) is an isometry,
\[
\{\beta\in R:(wv,\beta)>0\}
=w\{\gamma\in R:(v,\gamma)>0\}.
\]
A linear bijection preserving these positive sets preserves whether a root is a sum of two positive roots. By the equivalence in P.1, their indecomposable roots are respectively \(\Delta\) and \(w\Delta'\). Thus \(w\Delta'=\Delta\); its inverse is the required element. This finite-orbit maximization is the same elementary mechanism used for the reversing element in [De Rham decomposition AD.5](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#lemma-ad-5).

An isometry preserves every ratio in (O.2). Reordering the basis only permutes its indices simultaneously. The matrix and diagram conclusions follow. □

**Corollary P.3 (classification without a chosen basis).** Up to permutation of components and a positive scale on each component, reduced crystallographic root systems are classified by finite multisets of connected diagrams
\[
A_n\ (n\geq1),\quad B_n\ (n\geq2),\quad C_n\ (n\geq3),
\quad D_n\ (n\geq4),\quad E_6,E_7,E_8,F_4,G_2.
\tag{P.4}
\]
The omitted name \(C_2\) is \(B_2\). If rank zero is allowed, its unique root system is \(R=\varnothing\) in \(V=0\), corresponding to the empty multiset.

**Proof.** Choose a simple basis using P.1. Its row Cartan matrix satisfies (O.1): the nonpositive off-diagonal entries and integrality are proved in [De Rham decomposition AE.1](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#lemma-ae-1), with the denominator convention transposed. For \(d_i=\|\alpha_i\|^2/2\), its symmetrization is the positive Gram matrix. Apply O.2 to its connected blocks. P.1 identifies those blocks with the intrinsic orthogonal irreducible components, and P.2 shows that the diagram multiset is independent of the basis.

Every such multiset is realized by the direct-sum construction in P.1. If two multisets agree, choose and order bases with the same block Cartan matrix. P.1 gives a similarity on each block taking every root to a root, so the systems are equivalent.

Conversely an equivalence sends a simple basis to a simple basis: root coefficient expansions are preserved by the linear map, and positive component scales preserve all the Cartan ratios. The resulting matrices agree in the transported order. Applying P.2 to any other choice of basis proves equality of the diagram multisets. O.2 proves that the only duplicated name in its original table was \(B_2=C_2\), so (P.4) has no further identifications. In dimension zero, the absence of nonzero vectors forces the stated empty system. This completes the classification of these root systems. □

This classification concerns Euclidean root systems. A classification of Lie algebras or of Hermitian symmetric pairs requires the additional constructions connecting those objects with roots.

## Q. Roots of an arbitrary complex semisimple algebra

In this part \(\mathfrak g\) is a finite-dimensional **complex** semisimple Lie algebra. Semisimplicity means the absence of nonzero solvable ideals. Its Killing form is
\[
K(X,Y)=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathfrak g}(\operatorname{ad}X\,\operatorname{ad}Y).
\tag{Q.1}
\]
The complete algebraic foundations are in [Symmetric spaces H.1–H.6](symmetric-spaces.md#lemma-h-1): polynomial Jordan decomposition, Lie triangularization, Engel's theorem, the Killing criterion and inner derivations. Complete reducibility for all finite-dimensional semisimple modules is proved in [Symmetric spaces I.3](symmetric-spaces.md#corollary-i-3). No compact real form is assumed here.

**Lemma Q.1 (all finite-dimensional rank-one modules).** Suppose endomorphisms \(E,F,H\) of a finite-dimensional complex vector space satisfy
\[
[H,E]=2E,\qquad [H,F]=-2F,\qquad [E,F]=H.
\tag{Q.2}
\]
Their representation is a direct sum of irreducible strings \(V_m\), \(m\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0}\). Each has basis \(v_0,\ldots,v_m\) with
\[
\begin{aligned}
Hv_j&=(m-2j)v_j,\\
Fv_j&=v_{j+1}\quad(j<m),& Fv_m&=0,\\
Ev_0&=0,& Ev_j&=j(m-j+1)v_{j-1}\quad(j\geq1).
\end{aligned}
\tag{Q.3}
\]
In particular \(H\) is diagonalizable with integer eigenvalues, and \(E,F\) are nilpotent. Every irreducible string has one-dimensional weight spaces and is uniquely determined up to isomorphism by \(m\).

**Proof.** The trace-zero two-by-two matrices have basis
\[
e=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
f=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
h=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Their matrix commutators are (Q.2). The adjoint matrices give
\[
K(h,h)=8,\qquad K(e,f)=K(f,e)=4,
\]
with all other basis pairings zero. Indeed \(\operatorname{ad}h\) has eigenvalues \(2,-2,0\); the product \(\operatorname{ad}e\,\operatorname{ad}f\) has diagonal coefficients \(2,0,2\) on \(e,f,h\), respectively. The other traces follow by the same three commutators. The resulting Gram determinant is \(-128\), so [Symmetric spaces H.5](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-h-5) proves that this algebra \(\mathfrak{sl}_2(\mathbb C)\) is semisimple.

Relations (Q.2) define a representation of this three-dimensional algebra. [Symmetric spaces I.3](symmetric-spaces.md#corollary-i-3) decomposes every such module into irreducible submodules. It remains to determine an irreducible nonzero one.

By [Symmetric spaces H.1](symmetric-spaces.md#lemma-h-1), \(H\) has a nonempty finite eigenvalue set. There is an eigenvalue \(\lambda\) for which \(\lambda+2\) is absent: starting from any eigenvalue and repeatedly adding two must eventually leave that finite set. Choose an eigenvector \(v_0\) at such a \(\lambda\). The first commutator makes \(Ev_0\) an eigenvector at \(\lambda+2\) if it is nonzero, so \(Ev_0=0\). Put \(v_j=F^jv_0\). Induction gives
\[
Hv_j=(\lambda-2j)v_j,\qquad
Ev_j=j(\lambda-j+1)v_{j-1}.
\tag{Q.4}
\]
For the second identity commute \(E\) past the first \(F\) using \(EF=FE+H\). The coefficient becomes
\((j-1)(\lambda-j+2)+\lambda-2j+2=j(\lambda-j+1)\).

The nonzero \(v_j\) have distinct eigenvalues and hence are independent, by the polynomial projections in [Symmetric spaces H.1](symmetric-spaces.md#lemma-h-1). There is a last nonzero \(v_m\). The next identity in (Q.4) says
\[
0=(m+1)(\lambda-m)v_m,
\]
so \(\lambda=m\). All earlier vectors are nonzero, since one zero would make every subsequent \(F\)-iterate zero. Their span is invariant under all three operators and is therefore the entire irreducible module. This gives (Q.3).

Conversely (Q.3) defines operators satisfying (Q.2), as substitution at each \(v_j\), including \(j=0,m\), verifies. The resulting module is irreducible: an invariant subspace is preserved by the polynomial projections onto the distinct \(H\)-eigenlines. A nonzero subspace thus contains some \(v_j\); repeated \(E\), whose coefficients before \(v_0\) are nonzero, reaches \(v_0\), and repeated \(F\) reaches every basis vector. The displayed matrices also give uniqueness. The direct-sum decomposition now proves all assertions for arbitrary modules, including the zero module. □

An element of \(\mathfrak g\) is called **semisimple** when its adjoint operator is diagonalizable, and **nilpotent** when that operator is nilpotent. A **toral subalgebra** is an abelian subalgebra of semisimple elements. Here a **Cartan subalgebra** means a toral subalgebra equal to its centralizer. A Cartan is automatically maximal toral, since any larger toral subalgebra would centralize it.

**Theorem Q.2 (Cartan existence and the root decomposition).** Every maximal toral subalgebra \(\mathfrak h\) is a Cartan subalgebra. Such subalgebras exist; if \(\mathfrak g\ne0\), they are nonzero. There is a finite decomposition
\[
\begin{gathered}
\mathfrak g=\mathfrak h\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R}\mathfrak g_\alpha,\\
\mathfrak g_\alpha=\{X:[T,X]=\alpha(T)X\text{ for all }T\in\mathfrak h\},
\qquad R\subset\mathfrak h^*\setminus\{0\}.
\end{gathered}
\tag{Q.5}
\]
The roots \(R\) span the complex dual \(\mathfrak h^*\). Put \(\mathfrak g_0=\mathfrak h\), and interpret a missing nonzero weight space as zero. Then
\[
[\mathfrak g_\alpha,\mathfrak g_\beta]\subseteq\mathfrak g_{\alpha+\beta}.
\tag{Q.6}
\]
The Killing form is nondegenerate on \(\mathfrak h\) and on every opposite pairing \(\mathfrak g_\alpha\times\mathfrak g_{-\alpha}\); all other weight-space pairings are zero.

**Proof.** [Symmetric spaces H.5–H.6](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-h-5) give nondegeneracy of \(K\), zero centre, and commuting Jordan parts \(X=X_s+X_n\) inside \(\mathfrak g\). These parts have the adjoint Jordan operators. If \(Y\) commutes with \(X\), then \(\operatorname{ad}Y\) commutes with their polynomial expressions in \(\operatorname{ad}X\). Zero centre consequently gives \([Y,X_s]=[Y,X_n]=0\).

If every element had nilpotent adjoint operator, Engel's theorem, [Symmetric spaces H.4](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-h-4), would make \(\mathfrak g\) nilpotent and hence solvable. A nonzero semisimple algebra cannot have this property. Thus when \(\mathfrak g\ne0\), some \(X_s\ne0\) supplies a one-dimensional toral subalgebra. Increase its dimension while possible; finite dimension gives a maximal one. More generally every given toral subalgebra lies in a maximal one by the same argument.

For any toral \(\mathfrak h\), a finite basis gives commuting diagonalizable adjoint operators. Simultaneous diagonalization from [Symmetric spaces H.1](symmetric-spaces.md#lemma-h-1) yields a joint weight decomposition, initially with zero space \(\mathfrak z=C_{\mathfrak g}(\mathfrak h)\). Jacobi gives (Q.6). Killing invariance gives
\[
(\alpha(T)+\beta(T))K(X,Y)=0
\quad(X\in\mathfrak g_\alpha,\ Y\in\mathfrak g_\beta).
\tag{Q.7}
\]
If the two weights do not add to zero, some \(T\) makes that coefficient nonzero. Nondegeneracy on the whole algebra then implies nondegeneracy on \(\mathfrak z\) and on each opposite pairing: a vector annihilating its only possible partner space would annihilate all of \(\mathfrak g\).

Suppose now that \(\mathfrak h\) is maximal toral. For \(X\in\mathfrak z\), the Jordan parts still lie in \(\mathfrak z\) by the first paragraph. Since \(X_s\) commutes with \(\mathfrak h\), adjoining it produces an abelian algebra of commuting diagonalizable operators, all of whose linear combinations are diagonalizable. Maximality therefore gives \(X_s\in\mathfrak h\). On \(\mathfrak z\) its adjoint is zero, so \(\operatorname{ad}X|_{\mathfrak z}=\operatorname{ad}X_n|_{\mathfrak z}\) is nilpotent. Engel's theorem makes \(\mathfrak z\) nilpotent, hence solvable.

Apply Lie's theorem, [Symmetric spaces H.3](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-h-3), to the representation of this solvable algebra on \(\mathfrak g\). In a common triangular basis \(\operatorname{ad}X_n\) has zero diagonal, because it is nilpotent on the entire algebra. For every \(Y\in\mathfrak z\), the product \(\operatorname{ad}X_n\operatorname{ad}Y\) therefore has trace zero. Thus \(K(X_n,\mathfrak z)=0\). The restricted nondegeneracy already proved forces \(X_n=0\). Every \(X\in\mathfrak z\) consequently belongs to \(\mathfrak h\), proving \(\mathfrak z=\mathfrak h\) and (Q.5).

If \(T\in\mathfrak h\) is annihilated by all roots, it commutes with every summand of (Q.5), so is central and zero. The roots thus span \(\mathfrak h^*\): if their span were proper, solving the corresponding homogeneous linear equations would give a nonzero annihilator. For \(\mathfrak g\ne0\), the root set is nonempty, since otherwise \(\mathfrak g=\mathfrak h\) would be a nonzero abelian algebra. This proves the theorem. □

**Theorem Q.3 (coroot triples, root dimensions and reducedness).** Define \(T_\alpha\in\mathfrak h\) by
\[
K(T_\alpha,T)=\alpha(T)\quad(T\in\mathfrak h).
\tag{Q.8}
\]
Then \(q_\alpha=\alpha(T_\alpha)\ne0\). There are vectors \(e_\alpha\in\mathfrak g_\alpha\), \(f_\alpha\in\mathfrak g_{-\alpha}\) such that
\[
h_\alpha=\frac{2T_\alpha}{q_\alpha},\qquad
[h_\alpha,e_\alpha]=2e_\alpha,\quad
[h_\alpha,f_\alpha]=-2f_\alpha,\quad
[e_\alpha,f_\alpha]=h_\alpha.
\tag{Q.9}
\]
They span a copy of \(\mathfrak{sl}_2(\mathbb C)\). Every root space is one-dimensional. If \(c\alpha\in R\) for \(c\in\mathbb C\), then \(c=1\) or \(c=-1\). Moreover \(\beta(h_\alpha)\in\mathbb Z\) for all roots \(\alpha,\beta\).

**Proof.** Equation (Q.8) has a unique solution by Q.2. For \(e\in\mathfrak g_\alpha,f\in\mathfrak g_{-\alpha}\), Killing invariance gives, for every \(T\in\mathfrak h\),
\[
K([e,f],T)=K(e,[f,T])=\alpha(T)K(e,f).
\]
Since \([e,f]\in\mathfrak h\), nondegeneracy there yields
\[
[e,f]=K(e,f)T_\alpha.
\tag{Q.10}
\]
Choose \(e,f\) with \(K(e,f)=1\), using the opposite pairing of Q.2.

If \(q_\alpha=0\), then \(T_\alpha\) commutes with \(e,f\). Their span with \(T_\alpha\) is a solvable algebra, with central derived algebra. Lie's theorem makes its adjoint operators on \(\mathfrak g\) simultaneously upper triangular. In particular
\(\operatorname{ad}T_\alpha=[\operatorname{ad}e,\operatorname{ad}f]\)
is strictly triangular and nilpotent. But \(T_\alpha\in\mathfrak h\) has diagonalizable adjoint, so that adjoint is zero. Zero centre gives \(T_\alpha=0\), contrary to the nonzero functional \(\alpha\) in (Q.8). Thus \(q_\alpha\ne0\).

Keep \(e_\alpha=e\) and put \(f_\alpha=2f/q_\alpha\). Equations (Q.8)–(Q.10) give (Q.9). The three vectors are independent because they belong to the distinct weights \(\alpha,-\alpha,0\) of (Q.5), and their nonzero brackets are those of the three-by-basis algebra in Q.1. This identifies the subalgebra with \(\mathfrak{sl}_2\). Apply Q.1 to its action on \(\mathfrak g\): \(\beta(h_\alpha)\), an eigenvalue of \(\operatorname{ad}h_\alpha\) on \(\mathfrak g_\beta\), is an integer.

To prove the dimensions without assuming reducedness, use the invariant subspace
\[
M=\mathfrak h\oplus
\bigoplus_{\gamma\in R\cap\mathbb C\alpha}\mathfrak g_\gamma
\tag{Q.11}
\]
for this rank-one action. Its weight-zero space is exactly \(\mathfrak h\): if \(\gamma=c\alpha\), its \(h_\alpha\)-weight is \(2c\), which is zero only for \(c=0\). The map
\[
\operatorname{ad}e_\alpha:\mathfrak h\longrightarrow M,\qquad
T\longmapsto-\alpha(T)e_\alpha
\]
has rank one. In Q.1's string decomposition, every nontrivial even string has a one-dimensional zero-weight space which raises nontrivially. Odd strings have no zero weight and trivial strings contribute zero to this rank. Hence precisely one nontrivial even string occurs. The already embedded triple is \(V_2\), so it is that unique even string.

It follows that the weight-two space of \(M\), namely \(\mathfrak g_\alpha\), has dimension one, and the weight-four space is zero. Thus \(2\alpha\notin R\). This argument applies to every root.

Finally suppose \(\gamma=c\alpha\in R\). The integrality just proved gives \(2c=\gamma(h_\alpha)\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}\), so \(c\) is real. By (Q.8), \(T_\gamma=cT_\alpha\) and \(q_\gamma=c^2q_\alpha\), hence \(h_\gamma=h_\alpha/c\). Integrality again gives \(2/c=\alpha(h_\gamma)\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}\). After changing the sign of \(\gamma\), assume \(c>0\). The positive integers \(2c,2/c\) multiply to four; therefore \(c\in\{1/2,1,2\}\). The double-root exclusion rules out two, and its application to \(\gamma\) rules out one half. Only \(c=1\) remains. This proves reducedness even for complex proportionality. □

**Theorem Q.4 (complete root strings and nonzero brackets).** For \(\beta\ne\pm\alpha\), the roots on \(\beta+\mathbb Z\alpha\) form one uninterrupted string
\[
\beta-r\alpha,\ldots,\beta,\ldots,\beta+q\alpha,\qquad
r,q\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0},
\tag{Q.12}
\]
whose endpoints satisfy
\[
r-q=\beta(h_\alpha).
\tag{Q.13}
\]
Whenever \(\alpha+\beta\) is a root,
\[
[\mathfrak g_\alpha,\mathfrak g_\beta]=\mathfrak g_{\alpha+\beta}\ne0.
\tag{Q.14}
\]
The linear map on \(\mathfrak h^*\)
\[
s_\alpha\lambda=\lambda-\lambda(h_\alpha)\alpha
\tag{Q.15}
\]
permutes \(R\).

**Proof.** Q.3 makes \(\alpha,\beta\) nonproportional under the stated hypothesis. The sum
\[
M_{\alpha,\beta}=\bigoplus_{\beta+j\alpha\in R}\mathfrak g_{\beta+j\alpha}
\]
is invariant under the \(\alpha\) triple, by (Q.6). Its distinct \(h_\alpha\)-weights are \(\beta(h_\alpha)+2j\), each with multiplicity one by Q.3, and all are integers of the same parity.

By Q.1 this module is a sum of irreducible strings. Two even strings would both contribute weight zero, contradicting multiplicity one. Two odd strings would both contribute weight one, with the same contradiction. Since every constituent has the module's single parity, precisely one constituent occurs. Its weights are consecutive with step two and symmetric about zero. As the weight belonging to \(\beta\) occurs, the indices are the complete interval \(-r,\ldots,q\). Adding the two endpoint weights gives
\[
\bigl(\beta(h_\alpha)-2r\bigr)
+\bigl(\beta(h_\alpha)+2q\bigr)=0,
\]
which is (Q.13).

Within the irreducible string the raising operator is nonzero except at its upper endpoint, by (Q.3). Thus if \(\alpha+\beta\) exists, the bracket with \(e_\alpha\) maps the one-dimensional \(\beta\)-space nontrivially to the one-dimensional \(\alpha+\beta\)-space, proving (Q.14). Proportional roots cause no missing case in that assertion: reducedness excludes \(2\alpha\), and \(\alpha+(-\alpha)=0\) is not a root.

Negating a string weight sends the index \(j=0\) to the integer index \(-\beta(h_\alpha)\). Thus \(\beta-\beta(h_\alpha)\alpha\) is a root. For \(\beta=\pm\alpha\), (Q.15) exchanges \(\alpha\) and \(-\alpha\). These cases prove reflection closure. The map has square one because \(\alpha(h_\alpha)=2\), so it permutes the whole finite set. □

**Theorem Q.5 (the positive real Cartan space and its root system).** Let
\[
\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}
\{h_\alpha:\alpha\in R\}.
\tag{Q.16}
\]
Then
\[
\mathfrak h=\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\oplus i\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R},
\]
and \(K|_{\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}}\) is real and positive definite. The restrictions of \(R\) to the real dual \(E=\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}^*\), with the inverse Killing inner product, form a finite reduced crystallographic root system. In that Euclidean product,
\[
h_\alpha=\frac{2T_\alpha}{(\alpha,\alpha)},\qquad
\beta(h_\alpha)=\frac{2(\beta,\alpha)}{(\alpha,\alpha)}.
\tag{Q.17}
\]
Its simple bases and its finite-type diagram classification are those of P.1–P.3. For \(\mathfrak g=0\), all these spaces are zero and \(R\) is empty.

**Proof.** The roots span the complex dual by Q.2, so their Killing-dual vectors \(T_\alpha\), and consequently their nonzero scalar multiples \(h_\alpha\), span \(\mathfrak h\) over \(\mathbb C\). Every root takes real values on \(\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\), since its values on the spanning coroots are integers by Q.3. If \(X\in\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\cap i\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\), every root value on \(X\) is both real and purely imaginary, hence zero. Q.2 then gives \(X=0\). Together with complex spanning this proves the direct-sum assertion.

The simultaneous diagonalization in (Q.5), with root dimensions from Q.3, gives for \(X,Y\in\mathfrak h\)
\[
K(X,Y)=\sum_{\alpha\in R}\alpha(X)\alpha(Y).
\tag{Q.18}
\]
On \(\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\) all summands are real. At a nonzero \(X\), some root value is nonzero, again by Q.2, so \(K(X,X)\) is a strictly positive sum of real squares.

The real restrictions are distinct and nonzero: a complex-linear functional on \(\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\oplus i\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}\) is determined by its restriction to the first summand. They span \(E\), because their real common annihilator is zero. For any \(\alpha\), solve its dual equation using the real positive Gram matrix of \(K|_{\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}}\); this gives a real vector whose complexification satisfies (Q.8). Uniqueness in (Q.8) identifies it with \(T_\alpha\). Hence
\[
q_\alpha=\alpha(T_\alpha)=K(T_\alpha,T_\alpha)=(\alpha,\alpha)>0,
\]
and (Q.17) follows.

The set \(R\) is finite and spans \(E\); Q.3 gives reducedness and the integer Cartan numbers in (Q.17); Q.4 gives reflection closure. Formula (Q.15), together with (Q.17), is exactly the Euclidean orthogonal reflection (P.1). Thus all axioms of the root system in Part P have now been verified, and P.1–P.3 apply with their complete programme proofs.

If \(\mathfrak g\ne0\), Q.2 makes the Cartan subalgebra and the roots nonzero. For the zero algebra the stated choices verify every assertion directly. This positive real form is a real form of the Cartan vector space; it is not a compact real form of the whole Lie algebra. □

## R. Independence of the Cartan subalgebra

**Theorem R.1 (Cartan conjugacy and invariant Euclidean root data).** Any two Cartan subalgebras of a complex semisimple \(\mathfrak g\) are carried to one another by a finite product of automorphisms \(\exp(\operatorname{ad}X)\), \(X\in\mathfrak g\). Such an automorphism maps their canonical real Cartan spaces of Q.5 onto one another and identifies their root systems isometrically for the inverse Killing metrics. In particular the root data and diagram are independent of the Cartan choice.

**Proof.** The zero algebra is immediate, so suppose \(\mathfrak g\ne0\). We first give the elementary facts about complex polynomials used to connect the relevant elements.

For a nonzero polynomial \(p\) on \(\mathbb C^N\), the set \(U_p=\{p\ne0\}\) is open, dense and path connected. Continuity gives openness. A polynomial vanishing on a nonempty open set is zero: put a product of open discs inside that set, regard the polynomial as a polynomial in the first variable, and use that a nonzero one-variable polynomial has only finitely many roots. Its coefficient polynomials vanish on the remaining product; induction on \(N\) finishes the argument. The one-variable fact follows from division by a linear factor, proved in [Symmetric spaces H.1](symmetric-spaces.md#lemma-h-1). This proves density of \(U_p\).

For path connectedness, if \(x,y\in U_p\), the one-variable polynomial
\[
t\longmapsto p((1-t)x+ty)
\]
is not zero, since its values at zero and one are nonzero. Its zero set is finite. In the complex \(t\)-plane one can join zero to one while avoiding that set: start with the real segment and, around each zero on it, replace a small subsegment by a semicircle in a small disc containing no other zero and neither endpoint. The resulting continuous piecewise smooth path maps into \(U_p\). This proves all three facts.

Write the characteristic polynomial of \(\operatorname{ad}X\) as
\[
\det(tI-\operatorname{ad}X)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}c_j(X)t^j,\qquad
N=\dim_{\mathbb C}\mathfrak g.
\tag{R.1}
\]
Its coefficients are polynomial functions of the coordinates of \(X\), by the finite determinant expansion. Let \(r\) be the smallest multiplicity of the root zero among these polynomials as \(X\) varies. Thus \(c_j\) vanishes identically for \(j<r\), while \(c_r\) is not the zero polynomial. The set
\[
U=\{X:c_r(X)\ne0\}
\tag{R.2}
\]
consists exactly of the elements whose adjoint has zero eigenvalue with multiplicity \(r\), counting generalized eigenvectors. The preceding polynomial argument makes \(U\) nonempty, open, dense and connected.

Let \(\Gamma\) be the group of finite products of \(\exp(\operatorname{ad}X)\). These exponentials are automorphisms: for any derivation \(D\), differentiation gives
\[
\frac{d}{dt}\bigl(e^{-tD}[e^{tD}Y,e^{tD}Z]\bigr)=0,
\]
using the derivation rule, so the bracket is preserved at \(t=1\). Smoothness of the exponential and its derivative at zero are supplied by [Local tools 2.3](local-tools-for-bundles-and-transport.md#2-differential-equations-and-their-parameters), applied to the matrix group. Inverses are obtained by replacing \(X\) by \(-X\). Automorphisms conjugate adjoint operators and preserve every coefficient in (R.1), so \(\Gamma\) preserves \(U\).

Fix a Cartan \(\mathfrak h\) of dimension \(d\). Choose \(H\in\mathfrak h\) with \(\alpha(H)\ne0\) for every root. The finite-hyperplane argument in [De Rham decomposition AD.5](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#lemma-ad-5) applies over \(\mathbb C\) as well: when varying a vector along \(tu\), each nonconstant affine root value forbids at most one scalar \(t\). Thus such choices exist. Put \(\mathfrak m=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R}\mathfrak g_\alpha\), and consider
\[
\Phi:\mathfrak m\times\mathfrak h\longrightarrow\mathfrak g,\qquad
\Phi(Z,T)=\exp(\operatorname{ad}Z)T.
\tag{R.3}
\]
At \((0,H)\) its derivative is
\[
D\Phi_{(0,H)}(Z,T)=[Z,H]+T.
\]
On each root summand the first term is \(-\alpha(H)Z\), an invertible scalar, and on \(\mathfrak h\) the second term is the identity. Thus the derivative is an isomorphism. Apply the smooth real inverse function theorem, [Local tools 1.2](local-tools-for-bundles-and-transport.md#1-contraction-and-local-inversion), to the underlying real vector spaces. A small neighbourhood of \((0,H)\), restricted so that every root remains nonzero on \(T\), maps onto an open neighbourhood of \(H\).

Every element in that image is conjugate to such a \(T\); (Q.5) shows that its zero eigenvalue multiplicity is \(d\). This open image meets the dense set \(U\), forcing \(d=r\). The argument works for every Cartan, so all Cartan subalgebras have dimension \(r\).

Now let \(X\in U\). Its Jordan semisimple part \(X_s\) lies in some maximal toral \(\mathfrak h\): start with \(\mathbb C X_s\), which is toral even if it is zero, and use Q.2. The Jordan decomposition of \(\operatorname{ad}X\) shows that \(\operatorname{ad}X_s\) has the same eigenvalues with the same algebraic multiplicities. Its zero eigenspace therefore has dimension \(r\). It contains \(\mathfrak h\), whose dimension is \(r\), so
\[
C_{\mathfrak g}(X_s)=\mathfrak h.
\tag{R.4}
\]
The commuting nilpotent part \(X_n\) lies in this Cartan. Its adjoint is consequently both nilpotent and diagonalizable, hence zero. Zero centre gives \(X_n=0\). Thus every \(X\in U\) is semisimple and has Cartan centralizer \(C_{\mathfrak g}(X)=\mathfrak h\).

This Cartan is unique among those containing \(X\): any such Cartan lies in \(C_{\mathfrak g}(X)\) and has the same dimension \(r\). Conversely each Cartan contains an \(H\) as used in (R.3), for which the zero multiplicity is \(r\); hence every Cartan occurs as \(C_{\mathfrak g}(H)\) with \(H\in U\).

Partition \(U\) by \(\Gamma\)-conjugacy of these centralizers. Each class is open in \(U\). Indeed, around any \(H\in U\) with Cartan centralizer \(\mathfrak h\), the inverse-function neighbourhood (R.3) consists of elements conjugate to root-regular \(T\in\mathfrak h\), whose centralizer is exactly \(\mathfrak h\). Thus their centralizers are all conjugate to that of \(H\). If the partition had more than one class, one class and the union of all others would be disjoint nonempty open subsets separating \(U\). Its connectedness excludes this. There is one class, and since every Cartan occurs, they are all conjugate by \(\Gamma\).

Finally an automorphism \(F\) preserves \(K\), because
\(\operatorname{ad}(FX)=F(\operatorname{ad}X)F^{-1}\)
and trace is invariant under conjugation. If \(F\mathfrak h=\mathfrak h'\), it carries \(\mathfrak g_\alpha\) onto the root space for
\(\alpha'=\alpha\circ F^{-1}|_{\mathfrak h'}\).
Equation (Q.8) then gives \(F T_\alpha=T_{\alpha'}\), and (Q.9) gives \(Fh_\alpha=h_{\alpha'}\). Taking real spans proves \(F\mathfrak h_{\mathbb R}=\mathfrak h'_{\mathbb R}\). Its restriction preserves the real Killing inner product, so the induced dual map is an isometry carrying every root to its corresponding root. Part P now supplies the basis-independent diagram. □

## S. Words and the unreduced Lie presentation

In Parts S–U the field is \(\mathbb C\). Formal Lie algebras may initially be infinite dimensional; finiteness will be a conclusion. Fix an integral \(r\times r\) matrix \(A=(a_{ij})\) satisfying (O.1), including a positive diagonal \(D\) for which \(DA\) is symmetric positive definite. The empty matrix is allowed. We use the row convention
\[
\alpha_j(h_i)=a_{ij}.
\tag{S.1}
\]

**Lemma S.1 (formal free Lie algebras and ordered words).** A finite set \(X\) has a free Lie algebra \(F(X)\). It embeds in the free associative algebra \(T(X)\), with Lie bracket \(uv-vu\). More generally, if a complex Lie algebra \(L\) has a finite or countable ordered vector-space basis \((b_i)\), its enveloping algebra
\[
U(L)=T(L)/(xy-yx-[x,y]:x,y\in L)
\tag{S.2}
\]
has basis \(1\) and the words \(b_{i_1}\cdots b_{i_n}\) with \(i_1\leq\cdots\leq i_n\). In particular \(L\to U(L)\) is injective.

**Proof.** Take the vector space on all finite fully bracketed words in \(X\). Bracketing two words and extending bilinearly gives a nonassociative algebra. Quotient by the smallest subspace stable under bracketing on either side which contains antisymmetry and Jacobi expressions. The resulting bracket is a Lie bracket. Any map from \(X\) to a Lie algebra evaluates bracketed words, annihilates this subspace, and factors uniquely through the quotient. This constructs \(F(X)\) and its universal property. Its bracketed words form a countable spanning set; retaining a word precisely when it is not in the span of previously retained words gives a finite or countable basis.

For (S.2), \(T(L)\) is the vector space on ordinary words in basis letters, with concatenation as multiplication and the empty word as \(1\). Replace any adjacent descending pair by
\[
b_jb_i\ \longmapsto\ b_ib_j+[b_j,b_i]\qquad(j>i),
\tag{S.3}
\]
expanding the bracket in the basis. Order words first by length and then by the number of inverted pairs of indices. Each resulting term has smaller measure: the swapped term loses one inversion, and every bracket term has shorter length. Every expansion is finite; the length is bounded by the original length and the inversion count at each length is bounded by \(\binom n2\). Thus repeated reductions terminate.

We prove by induction on this measure that the resulting linear combination of ordered words is independent of all reduction choices. Two disjoint adjacent reductions commute after distributing sums, and the induction hypothesis applies to every resulting smaller word. The only overlapping choices have three letters \(c>b>a\). Reducing the three-letter part in the two orders gives, before reducing the shorter terms,
\[
\begin{aligned}
abc+[b,a]c+b[c,a]+[c,b]a,\\
abc+a[c,b]+[c,a]b+c[b,a].
\end{aligned}
\]
Their difference reduces to
\[
[[b,a],c]+[b,[c,a]]+[[c,b],a]=0.
\tag{S.4}
\]
Indeed the difference is the sum of three two-factor commutators; (S.3), bilinearity and antisymmetry reduce each to its bracket. With any words before and after these three letters, all these correction terms have strictly smaller total length than the original word. The induction hypothesis therefore justifies those reductions even in their surrounding context. Jacobi proves the last equality. These are all possible pairs of distinct first choices. If the first choice is the same, induction applies to its terms. This completes the induction and defines a unique linear normal-form map.

The map kills each expression \(p(xy-yx-[x,y])q\). For basis vectors this is one reduction, or its reverse when their indices increase, or zero for equal indices; uniqueness gives the same normal form in every context \(p,q\). Bilinearity gives the assertion for arbitrary \(x,y\). It therefore kills the entire defining two-sided ideal. Ordered words are fixed by the map and are linearly independent before quotienting. They consequently remain independent in the quotient; reductions show they span it. Length-one words prove that \(L\to U(L)\) is injective.

The commutator in any associative algebra is a Lie bracket: antisymmetry is immediate, and expanding Jacobi cancels the six ordered triple products in opposite pairs. An associative algebra map from \(U(L)\) is determined exactly by a linear map on \(L\) preserving brackets as commutators: evaluate tensor words, and (S.2) is precisely the condition for factorization. Apply this and the free-Lie universal property to \(F(X)\). There are maps
\[
T(X)\longrightarrow U(F(X))\longrightarrow T(X)
\]
fixing all letters of \(X\). Their composites are identities: \(T(X)\) is generated associatively by \(X\), and \(U(F(X))\) is generated by \(F(X)\), whose elements are Lie expressions in \(X\). Hence \(U(F(X))\cong T(X)\). The established injection of \(F(X)\) into its enveloping algebra gives the asserted free-Lie embedding. This proof also covers \(X=\varnothing\), for which \(F(X)=0\). □

**Lemma S.2 (independent Cartan generators and a free triangular decomposition).** Let \(\widetilde{\mathfrak g}_A\) be the Lie algebra on \(e_i,f_i,h_i\), subject only to
\[
\begin{gathered}
[h_i,h_j]=0,\qquad [h_i,e_j]=a_{ij}e_j,\\
[h_i,f_j]=-a_{ij}f_j,\qquad [e_i,f_j]=\delta_{ij}h_i.
\end{gathered}
\tag{S.5}
\]
Then the \(h_i\) are independent, and, as vector spaces,
\[
\widetilde{\mathfrak g}_A
=F(f_1,\ldots,f_r)\ \oplus\ \mathfrak h\
\oplus\ F(e_1,\ldots,e_r),
\qquad \mathfrak h=\bigoplus_i\mathbb C h_i.
\tag{S.6}
\]
All three summands embed by their indicated generators.

**Proof.** The presentation exists by S.1: take the free Lie algebra on the \(3r\) symbols and quotient by the ideal of (S.5). Give these symbols degrees \(-\varepsilon_i,0,\varepsilon_i\) for \(f_i,h_i,e_i\), respectively. The relations are homogeneous for this \(\mathbb Z^r\)-grading, which therefore descends to the quotient.

Write \(\widetilde{\mathfrak n}_\pm\) for the subalgebras generated by the corresponding signs. Jacobi and (S.5) show that \(\operatorname{ad}h_i\) preserves them. Induction on the length of a positive bracketed word gives
\[
[f_i,\widetilde{\mathfrak n}_+]\subset
\mathfrak h+\widetilde{\mathfrak n}_+.
\]
At length one this is (S.5); at a bracket of two shorter positive words apply Jacobi, and commute any resulting \(h_j\) past the remaining positive word. Interchanging signs gives the analogous inclusion for \([e_i,\widetilde{\mathfrak n}_-]\). Consequently the sum of the three indicated spaces is stable under the adjoint action of every generator and contains the generators. Repeated use of \([[x,y],z]=[x,[y,z]]-[y,[x,z]]\) expresses any bracketed word as a sum of iterated adjoint actions of generators on a generator. The three spaces thus span the presented algebra.

To show that they have no hidden relations, let \(T(f)\) be the free associative algebra on the \(f\)-letters and put
\[
\mathcal V=T(f)\otimes\mathbb C[t_1,\ldots,t_r].
\]
For a word \(w=f_{j_1}\cdots f_{j_s}\), set
\(\mu_i(w)=\sum_{\nu=1}^s a_{i j_\nu}\).
Define polynomial-linear operators, writing the tensor product as \(wP(t)\), by
\[
\begin{aligned}
F_i(wP)&=f_iwP,\\
H_i(wP)&=w(t_i-\mu_i(w))P,\\
E_i(1P)&=0,\\
E_i(f_jwP)&=f_jE_i(wP)+\delta_{ij}H_i(wP).
\end{aligned}
\tag{S.7}
\]
The last rule is a recursion on word length, so it defines \(E_i\) uniquely. The \(H_i\) commute, and the first two lines give \([H_i,F_j]=-a_{ij}F_j\). The recursion gives \([E_i,F_j]=\delta_{ij}H_i\). Every term of \(E_j(wP)\) is a polynomial times a word obtained by deleting one \(f_j\) from \(w\); its \(\mu_i\) is therefore \(\mu_i(w)-a_{ij}\). Computing \(H_iE_j-E_jH_i\) on each term gives \(a_{ij}E_j\). These are exactly all relations (S.5), so (S.7) defines a representation of \(\widetilde{\mathfrak g}_A\).

A Lie expression in the \(F_i\) acts by left multiplication by the corresponding commutator expression in \(T(f)\). A nonzero element of \(F(f)\) has a nonzero image there by S.1, and acting on the empty word detects it. Thus \(F(f)\to\widetilde{\mathfrak g}_A\) is injective. If \(\sum c_i h_i=0\), acting on \(1\) gives \(\sum c_i t_i=0\), so every \(c_i=0\).

The substitution
\[
\omega(e_i)=f_i,\qquad
\omega(f_i)=e_i,\qquad
\omega(h_i)=-h_i
\tag{S.8}
\]
preserves (S.5) and has square one. For example
\([f_i,e_j]=-\delta_{ij}h_i=\delta_{ij}\omega(h_i)\);
the \(h,e\) and \(h,f\) relations interchange with their required signs. Hence it is an automorphism, and it transfers the free injection on the negative side to the positive side. Positive words have degrees in \(\mathbb N^r\setminus\{0\}\), negative words have opposite degrees, and the \(h_i\) have degree zero. The grading makes their sum direct. This proves (S.6). □

## T. The Serre quotient and its finiteness

Set \(m_{ij}=1-a_{ij}\) for \(i\ne j\), and define
\[
S_{ij}^+=(\operatorname{ad}e_i)^{m_{ij}}e_j,\qquad
S_{ij}^-=(\operatorname{ad}f_i)^{m_{ij}}f_j.
\tag{T.1}
\]
Let \(\mathfrak g_A\) be the quotient of \(\widetilde{\mathfrak g}_A\) by all these elements.

**Lemma T.1 (the precise ideals on both sides).** If \(I_\pm\) is the ideal generated by the \(S_{ij}^\pm\) inside \(F(e)\) or \(F(f)\), respectively, then the ideal they generate in \(\widetilde{\mathfrak g}_A\) is exactly \(I_-\oplus I_+\). Consequently
\[
\mathfrak g_A=\mathfrak n_-\oplus\mathfrak h\oplus\mathfrak n_+,
\qquad
\mathfrak n_-=F(f)/I_-,\quad
\mathfrak n_+=F(e)/I_+.
\tag{T.2}
\]
The \(h_i\) remain independent and each \(e_i,f_i\) remains nonzero. Thus the relations (T.1) are precisely defining relations for the two indicated subalgebras.

**Proof.** We first compute in the unreduced algebra that
\[
[f_k,S_{ij}^+]=0\quad\hbox{for every }k.
\tag{T.3}
\]
For \(k\notin\{i,j\}\), \(f_k\) commutes with both letters. For \(k=i\), use \(E=\operatorname{ad}e_i\), \(F=\operatorname{ad}f_i\), \(H=\operatorname{ad}h_i\), and \(v=e_j\). They satisfy \([E,F]=H\), \([H,E]=2E\), \(Fv=0\), and \(Hv=a_{ij}v\). Expanding the commutator of \(F\) with \(E^m\) gives
\[
FE^m v
=-\sum_{\nu=0}^{m-1}E^\nu HE^{m-1-\nu}v
=-m(a_{ij}+m-1)E^{m-1}v.
\tag{T.4}
\]
Here \(HE^qv=(a_{ij}+2q)E^qv\), by induction from \([H,E]=2E\); summing the arithmetic progression gives the last equality. At \(m=m_{ij}\) its coefficient is zero. For \(k=j\), \(f_j\) commutes with \(e_i\), and hence
\[
[f_j,S_{ij}^+]=-(\operatorname{ad}e_i)^{m_{ij}}h_j.
\]
The first adjoint application gives \(-a_{ji}e_i\), and the second gives zero. If \(m_{ij}=1\), then \(a_{ij}=0\), so \(a_{ji}=0\) as well. This proves (T.3) in every case. The automorphism (S.8) gives \([e_k,S_{ij}^-]=0\).

Each \(S_{ij}^+\) is an \(h\)-weight vector. The positive ideal \(I_+\) is spanned by repeated adjoint actions of positive generators on these vectors: this span is stable under the generators, hence under all their bracketed words by Jacobi, and is the smallest such ideal. All those words are weight vectors, so \(I_+\) is \(h\)-stable. It is also stable under each \(\operatorname{ad}f_k\). Induct on the number of positive adjoint actions, starting with (T.3). In the step
\[
[f_k,[e_l,u]]
=[-\delta_{kl}h_l,u]+[e_l,[f_k,u]],
\]
both terms lie in \(I_+\), by weight stability and the induction hypothesis. Thus \(I_+\) is an ideal in the whole unreduced algebra. The same holds for \(I_-\) by (S.8).

Their sum is therefore an ideal containing all the relations (T.1), and every ideal containing those relations contains both \(I_+\) and \(I_-\). This proves equality with the full defining ideal. It is a direct sum by (S.6). Taking the quotient proves (T.2), including the exact positive and negative presentations. Each generator of \(I_+\) has positive total degree at least two; further positive brackets increase that degree. Thus \(I_+\) has no degree-\(\varepsilon_i\) part. The negative argument is identical. No part of either ideal has degree zero, so the \(h_i\) also survive independently.

Since \(A\) is invertible, the \(\alpha_j\) defined by (S.1) form a basis of \(\mathfrak h^*\). The multidegree \((c_j)\) corresponds to the weight \(\sum c_j\alpha_j\), because adjoint \(h_i\)-weights add under brackets. Distinct degrees therefore have distinct weights. Formula (T.2) says all nonzero weights have coefficients all nonnegative or all nonpositive, and the zero-weight space is exactly \(\mathfrak h\). Every fixed positive or negative degree has finite dimension: only finitely many bracketed words have that length and those letter multiplicities. Finally, a degree \(m\varepsilon_i\) with \(m\geq2\) is zero. Such words use just \(e_i\), or just \(f_i\), and every bracketed word in one letter of length at least two vanishes, by induction and \([e_i,e_i]=[f_i,f_i]=0\). □

**Lemma T.2 (reflection automorphisms before finiteness).** The derivations \(\operatorname{ad}e_i\) and \(\operatorname{ad}f_i\) are locally nilpotent on \(\mathfrak g_A\). The automorphism
\[
w_i=\exp(\operatorname{ad}e_i)
      \exp(-\operatorname{ad}f_i)
      \exp(\operatorname{ad}e_i)
\tag{T.5}
\]
satisfies
\[
w_i(e_i)=-f_i,\quad w_i(f_i)=-e_i,\quad
w_i(H)=H-\alpha_i(H)h_i\quad(H\in\mathfrak h).
\tag{T.6}
\]
It maps the weight space of \(\gamma\) isomorphically onto that of
\(\gamma-\gamma(h_i)\alpha_i\).

**Proof.** Write \(E=\operatorname{ad}e_i\). It kills \(e_i\), and (T.1) gives a vanishing power on each other \(e_j\). It sends \(h_j\) to \(-a_{ji}e_i\) and then to zero. It kills \(f_j\) for \(j\ne i\), while
\[
f_i\ \xmapsto{E}\ h_i\
\xmapsto{E}\ -2e_i\ \xmapsto{E}\ 0.
\]
It is consequently nilpotent on each generator. For any derivation \(N\), induction on \(q\) gives
\[
N^q[u,v]=\sum_{a=0}^q\binom qa[N^a u,N^{q-a}v].
\tag{T.7}
\]
If \(N^p u=N^s v=0\), every term vanishes for \(q\geq p+s-1\). Induction on bracketed words and then finite linear sums proves that \(E\) is locally nilpotent everywhere. The same argument, or (S.8), handles \(\operatorname{ad}f_i\).

For a locally nilpotent derivation \(N\), the exponential is a finite sum on each vector. Formula (T.7) proves \(\exp N[u,v]=[\exp Nu,\exp Nv]\); only finitely many terms occur for fixed \(u,v\). Multiplication of the finite power series on each such vector gives inverse \(\exp(-N)\). This proves that (T.5) is an automorphism without assuming finite dimensionality.

Put \(e=e_i,f=f_i,h=h_i\) and \(F=\operatorname{ad}f\). Directly from the three brackets \([h,e]=2e,[h,f]=-2f,[e,f]=h\),
\[
\begin{gathered}
\exp E(f)=f+h-e,\qquad \exp E(h)=h-2e,\\
\exp(-F)(e)=e+h-f,\qquad
\exp(-F)(h)=h-2f.
\end{gathered}
\]
For \(e\), the three factors in (T.5), acting from right to left, give \(e\), then \(e+h-f\), then \(-f\). For \(f\) they give \(f+h-e\), then \(-e\), then \(-e\). For a general \(H\in\mathfrak h\), write \(a=\alpha_i(H)\). The first factor to act sends it to \(H-ae\). Since \(\exp(-F)H=H-af\), the next sends this to \(H-ae-ah\), and the last sends it to \(H-ah\). This is (T.6).

The restriction of \(w_i\) to \(\mathfrak h\) is an involution, since \(\alpha_i(h_i)=2\). For a weight vector \(x\), compute
\[
[H,w_i x]=w_i[w_i^{-1}H,x]
=\bigl(\gamma(H)-\gamma(h_i)\alpha_i(H)\bigr)w_i x.
\]
The inverse gives equality of the indicated weight spaces, not just inclusion. Under the root realization P.1, \(\gamma(h_i)=2(\gamma,\alpha_i)/(\alpha_i,\alpha_i)\), so this is the simple-root reflection \(s_i\). □

**Theorem T.3 (exact finite root decomposition).** Let \(R_A\) be the finite root system of P.1 with ordered simple basis \(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_r\) and Cartan matrix \(A\). Then
\[
\mathfrak g_A=\mathfrak h\oplus
\bigoplus_{\gamma\in R_A}(\mathfrak g_A)_\gamma,
\qquad \dim(\mathfrak g_A)_\gamma=1,
\qquad \dim\mathfrak g_A=r+|R_A|.
\tag{T.8}
\]
The subalgebra \(\mathfrak h\) is maximal toral and self-centralizing, and its row Cartan matrix is \(A\).

**Proof.** By T.1 the possible nonzero weights lie in the positive or negative integral cone on the \(\alpha_i\). Identify their real span with the Euclidean realization P.1, whose Gram matrix is \(DA\). Suppose \(\gamma=\sum c_i\alpha_i\) is a positive weight with nonzero weight space. If just one coefficient is positive, T.1 forces \(\gamma=\alpha_i\), since every higher pure multiple has zero space.

Otherwise at least two coefficients are positive. Since
\[
0<(\gamma,\gamma)=\sum_i c_i(\gamma,\alpha_i),
\]
there is an \(i\) with \(c_i>0\) and \((\gamma,\alpha_i)>0\). The number
\[
k=\gamma(h_i)=\sum_j c_j a_{ij}
=\frac{2(\gamma,\alpha_i)}{(\alpha_i,\alpha_i)}
\]
is a positive integer. T.2 takes its nonzero space to the space of \(s_i\gamma=\gamma-k\alpha_i\). At least one other positive coefficient remains. Thus \(s_i\gamma\) cannot lie in the negative cone or be zero. The one-sign restriction of T.1 forces it into the positive cone; its height \(\sum c_i\) has decreased by \(k\). Repeating reaches a simple root after finitely many steps.

It follows that every positive weight lies in the simple-reflection orbit of a simple root. The negative argument follows by changing all signs, using the same descent on \(-\gamma\). P.1 proves that the union of these orbits is precisely the finite set \(R_A\). Conversely each simple-root vector \(e_i\) is nonzero by T.1, and every word of simple reflections is realized on weights by a word of the automorphisms \(w_i\). Hence every member of \(R_A\) actually occurs. This argument does not require relations among the \(w_i\): a chosen word of root reflections is enough.

The simple-root spaces are one dimensional, spanned by their single degree-one generators. Descent and the isomorphisms in T.2 therefore show that every root space is one dimensional. The zero space is exactly \(\mathfrak h\), so (T.8) follows. In particular the algebra is finite dimensional, now as a proved fact.

Its weight decomposition makes every \(\operatorname{ad}H\), \(H\in\mathfrak h\), diagonalizable, and the zero-space statement says \(C_{\mathfrak g_A}(\mathfrak h)=\mathfrak h\). Thus \(\mathfrak h\) is toral and cannot be contained in a larger toral subalgebra. The surviving triples \(e_i,f_i,h_i\) and (S.1) give the asserted row Cartan matrix. For \(r=0\), S.1 gives the zero algebra and all statements have their empty meanings. □

**Theorem T.4 (simplicity and the component decomposition).** If the diagram of \(A\) is nonempty and connected, \(\mathfrak g_A\) is simple. For arbitrary \(A\), it is the direct sum of the simple algebras belonging to the connected components, and is semisimple.

**Proof.** First suppose the diagram is connected. Let \(J\ne0\) be an ideal. Choose \(H\in\mathfrak h\) on which the finitely many distinct weights \(R_A\cup\{0\}\) take distinct values. Such \(H\) exists: the product of their nonzero pairwise differences is a nonzero polynomial, which cannot vanish everywhere, as proved in R.1. The projection onto any eigenspace of \(\operatorname{ad}H\) is the polynomial
\[
\prod_{\lambda\ne\mu}
\frac{\operatorname{ad}H-\lambda I}{\mu-\lambda},
\tag{T.9}
\]
where \(\lambda,\mu\) run over its distinct eigenvalues. The ideal is stable under these polynomials and hence is the sum of its weight intersections.

It therefore contains either a nonzero root vector or a nonzero \(Z\in\mathfrak h\). In the latter case some \(\alpha_i(Z)\ne0\), since the simple roots are a basis, and \([Z,e_i]=\alpha_i(Z)e_i\) supplies a root vector. Every exponential in (T.5) preserves \(J\), as does its inverse: they are finite sums of adjoint operators preserving the ideal. The descent in T.3 therefore carries a root vector in \(J\) to a nonzero multiple of a simple \(e_i\) or \(f_i\). In the latter case \(w_i\) sends it to a multiple of \(e_i\). The root-space dimensions show \(e_i\in J\).

Now \(h_i=[e_i,f_i]\in J\), and \([h_i,f_i]=-2f_i\) gives \(f_i\in J\). If vertex \(j\) is adjacent to \(i\), then \(a_{ij}\ne0\), so \([h_i,e_j]=a_{ij}e_j\) gives \(e_j\in J\), and then also \(h_j,f_j\in J\). Connectivity propagates this to every generator. Thus \(J=\mathfrak g_A\). The algebra is nonabelian because \(h_i\ne0\), so it is simple.

For different components all generator brackets vanish across the components. This is already (S.5) for mixed signs or an \(h\); for equal signs, \(a_{ij}=0\) makes the Serre relation precisely \([e_i,e_j]=0\) or \([f_i,f_j]=0\). Jacobi then proves that the generated component subalgebras commute. The presentation for each diagonal block maps into \(\mathfrak g_A\). Sending all generators outside that block to zero respects every defining relation and is a left inverse, so the map is injective. More explicitly, the map from \(\mathfrak g_A\) to the direct sum of the block presentations, defined on its generators, respects the same relations; its inverse is the sum of those commuting inclusions. Both composites fix all generators. This proves the asserted direct sum.

Each nonzero simple summand is perfect, because its derived algebra is a nonzero ideal; it is consequently not solvable. The image of a solvable ideal under projection to any summand is a solvable ideal there, hence zero. Thus the direct sum has no nonzero solvable ideal and is semisimple. The empty sum is the zero semisimple algebra. Applying Q.5 now also gives its canonical positive real Cartan form. The Euclidean realization with Gram matrix \(DA\) is equivalent to that root system by P.1, with the permitted positive scale on each component; no prescribed \(D\) was asserted to be the Killing normalization. □

## U. Reconstruction and classification of complex semisimple algebras
