It is one of the two stated choices on each nonflat factor, and a constant orthogonal complex structure on the Euclidean factor. The latter forces the Euclidean dimension to be even. These statements concern simply connected spaces; descent to any further quotient requires preservation of the complex structure.

**Proof.** A.2 makes the complex structure \(J\) of a Hermitian symmetric space parallel. Apply AJ.2 to its underlying Riemannian metric. The curvature action on a parallel endomorphism vanishes, by [Linear connections C.1](linear-and-affine-connections.md#theorem-c-1), so
\[
[R(X,Y),J]=0.
\tag{AJ.7}
\]
At the product base point, write the tangent decomposition as
\[
V=V_0\perp V_1\perp\cdots\perp V_s,
\]
where \(V_0\) is the Euclidean part. Its curvature algebra is
\(\mathfrak h_1\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak h_s\), with \(\mathfrak h_a\) acting only on \(V_a\). On a nonflat irreducible factor,
\[
[\mathfrak h_a,V_a]=V_a.
\tag{AJ.8}
\]
The left side is invariant; irreducibility makes it either zero or all of \(V_a\). If it were zero, (AJ.2) would give zero curvature, contradicting the definite Ricci tensor of D.1.

For \(A\in\mathfrak h_a\) and \(v\in V_a\), equation (AJ.7) says \(J(Av)=A(Jv)\), whose right side lies in \(V_a\). Equation (AJ.8) therefore gives \(J(V_a)\subseteq V_a\). Also \(V_0\) is exactly the common fixed space of all the \(\mathfrak h_a\): an irreducible nontrivial \(V_a\) has no fixed vectors. Commutation then gives \(J(V_0)\subseteq V_0\). Parallelism extends these statements from the base point to the product distributions.

On each nonflat factor, \(J|_{V_a}\) is an invariant orthogonal complex structure. E.2 and AI.1 give exactly the displayed family list and exactly the two opposite choices. In particular, both exceptional Hermitian factors occur as actual complete simply connected spaces by AJ.1, with complex dimensions 16 and 27 from AG.1. The other exceptional and the group-type factors are excluded by their centre-free even algebras.

On the Euclidean factor parallelism means that the matrix of \(J\) in constant coordinates is constant. Choose a unit vector \(v\). Orthogonality and \(J^2=-1\) give \(\langle v,Jv\rangle=0\) and \(|Jv|=1\). The plane spanned by \(v,Jv\) is \(J\)-stable, and its orthogonal complement is also stable: pairing \(Jw\) with a vector in the plane is the negative pairing of \(w\) with its \(J\)-image. Induction gives an orthonormal basis of such pairs. Hence its dimension is \(2d\), and these coordinates identify it isometrically and complex linearly with \(\mathbb C^d\).

Conversely choose the indicated algebraic complex structure on each listed factor. The isotropy \(H\) in (AJ.1) is connected, so infinitesimal invariance gives full \(H\)-invariance by exponentiation. A.3 constructs its global integrable complex structure and proves that the metric is Kähler and Hermitian symmetric. The Euclidean factor has its constant complex structure, and product complex charts give the product structure. It is parallel, orthogonal and preserved by the product point symmetries. All positive scales are allowed by D.1 and E.2.

Finally these assertions are compatible with the isometries used in AJ.2. Two parallel endomorphisms agreeing at the base point agree along every path; an affine isometry whose base differential intertwines the chosen complex structures therefore intertwines them everywhere. Such a smooth map is holomorphic by the complex-coordinate criterion used in A.2. For a further quotient, the structure descends precisely when every identifying transformation preserves it; in a symmetric presentation this is the full stabilizer condition A.3. Thus no disconnected-isotropy descent is implicit in the simply connected list. □

## AK. Special-unitary and oriented real models

The graph convention remains \(E_Z=\{(v,Zv)\}\), with \(Z\) of size \(q\times p\) and \(p,q\geq1\). Write
\[
\begin{aligned}
H_{\mathbb C}&=S(U(p)\times U(q))\\
&=\{\operatorname{diag}(a,d):a\in U(p),\ d\in U(q),\
                         \det a\,\det d=1\}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{AK.1}
\]

**Theorem AK.1 (The special-unitary quotient models).** The two homogeneous spaces
\[
SU(p+q)/H_{\mathbb C},\qquad SU(p,q)/H_{\mathbb C},
\quad SU(p,q)=U(p,q)\cap SL(p+q,\mathbb C),
\tag{AK.2}
\]
are respectively the complex Grassmannian of L.1 and the matrix ball of L.2, with precisely their complex structures and metrics. Both acting groups and the full stabilizer \(H_{\mathbb C}\) are connected. The graph representatives \(k_Z\) and \(g_Z\) in (L.4) and (L.14) have determinant one. The compact model is simply connected, as is the noncompact model. Their effective symmetric algebras are the compact and noncompact AIII pair, with the opposite brackets and metric normalization of L.3.

**Proof.** Here and below a block diagonal matrix is identified with its two diagonal blocks. First we verify the determinant assertion without taking a branch of a complex root. For either sign \(\epsilon=1,-1\), put
\[
P_\epsilon=I+\epsilon Z^*Z,\qquad Q_\epsilon=I+\epsilon ZZ^*,
\qquad
B_\epsilon=
\begin{pmatrix}I&-\epsilon Z^*\\ Z&I\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{AK.3}
\]
For the minus sign \(Z\) is in the ball. Multiplication on the left by
\(\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\-Z&I\end{pmatrix}\) gives the upper triangular block matrix with diagonal blocks \(I,Q_\epsilon\). The multiplying matrix has determinant one, so \(\det B_\epsilon=\det Q_\epsilon\). These determinant rules follow directly from the alternating multilinear determinant: a lower block row addition preserves it, and an upper triangular block matrix has the product of the diagonal determinants. By K.1, the positive spectra of \(Z^*Z\) and \(ZZ^*\), with multiplicities, agree; their remaining eigenvalues are zero. Consequently
\(\det P_\epsilon=\det Q_\epsilon>0\). Multiplying \(B_\epsilon\) on the right by
\(\operatorname{diag}(P_\epsilon^{-1/2},Q_\epsilon^{-1/2})\) gives exactly \(k_Z\) or \(g_Z\). Thus
\[
\det k_Z=\det g_Z
=\frac{\det Q_\epsilon}
       {\sqrt{\det P_\epsilon}\sqrt{\det Q_\epsilon}}=1,
\tag{AK.4}
\]
where each square root is positive, by the positive eigenvalues in K.1.

For the compact action, L.1 supplies a unitary matrix \(u\) sending the reference plane \(E\) to any chosen plane. Choose a block diagonal unitary matrix \(h\) with \(\det h=(\det u)^{-1}\): one entry in its first block can have this value and all other entries can be one. Then \(uh\in SU(p+q)\) has the same image plane. This proves transitivity. The full stabilizer is the intersection of the full stabilizer in L.1 with \(SU(p+q)\), namely (AK.1). For the noncompact action the determinant-one section \(g_Z\) already proves transitivity, and the stabilizer calculation in L.2 gives exactly the same intersection. These groups are closed matrix Lie subgroups by [Invariant connections A.1](invariant-connections-on-homogeneous-bundles.md#theorem-a-1), and [Local tools 4.1](local-tools-for-bundles-and-transport.md#4-quotients-by-a-closed-lie-subgroup) gives their smooth quotients. The graph sections just proved to lie in the smaller groups show that the quotient identifications and their inverses are smooth. In the compact case translate these graph sections by fixed elements of \(SU(p+q)\) to cover the space. They give the same holomorphic graph transitions as L.1. In the noncompact case the section is global. Thus the complex atlases and the metrics are exactly those already constructed in L.1 and L.2.

The ball can also be described intrinsically as the set of positive \(p\)-planes for the form \(\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\). The first projection is injective on any such plane: a nonzero vector in its kernel would have negative norm. Equal dimensions make the projection an isomorphism, so the plane is a unique graph \(E_Z\); its form is \(v^*(I-Z^*Z)v\), which is positive exactly for \(Z\) in the ball. Changing the sign of the preserved form makes the same planes negative and leaves the preserving group unchanged.

To check connectedness of the full stabilizer, diagonalize \(a\) and \(d\) separately by the commuting-Hermitian argument in K.1. List the eigenangles of both blocks as \(t_1,\ldots,t_{p+q}\). Their sum is \(2\pi k\) for an integer \(k\), by the determinant-one condition. Subtract \(2\pi k\) from one angle. This changes neither endpoint matrix and makes the total sum zero. In the two fixed diagonalizing bases, replace every angle by \(st_j\), \(0\leq s\leq1\). The two block paths have product determinant
\(\exp(is\sum_jt_j)=1\), and give a path in \(H_{\mathbb C}\) from the identity to \((a,d)\). The compact acting group is connected by K.1. For any \(g\in SU(p,q)\), let \(Z=g\cdot0\). Then \(g=g_Zh\) with \(h\in H_{\mathbb C}\), and \(s\mapsto g_{sZ}\), followed by a translated path in \(H_{\mathbb C}\), joins the identity to \(g\).

The involution induced by \(\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\) preserves each special-unitary group and has full fixed subgroup (AK.1). Its odd matrices are exactly (L.18); the even algebra is the trace-zero block diagonal algebra. Thus L.3 proves the asserted brackets, curvature and common tangent metric. This even action is faithful: if \(dZ=Za\) for every rectangular \(Z\), the matrix units first imply that \(a,d\) are scalar and then that their scalars agree. Total trace zero makes that scalar zero. The odd space generates the whole special-unitary symmetric algebra: brackets of matrix units give the off-diagonal entries in each diagonal block, their diagonal differences and the remaining trace-zero scalar combination. Alternatively this is the effective irreducible AIII pair already proved in AD.1 and AE.2. AC.1 proves that the compact special-unitary algebra has negative-definite Killing form. Hence the compact Hermitian space of L.1 is of compact type, and I.2 proves its simple connectivity. For the noncompact space the convex contraction in L.2 already proves it. These conclusions concern the actual quotients (AK.2), with their full stabilizers. □

Let \(\mathrm{Gr}^{+}_p(\mathbb R^{p+q})\) denote the space of real \(p\)-planes together with an orientation of the plane. An orientation of a real vector space means a choice of the ordered bases whose changes of basis have positive determinant.

**Theorem AK.2 (Oriented Grassmannians and real matrix balls).** There are smooth homogeneous identifications
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{Gr}^{+}_p(\mathbb R^{p+q})
 &=SO(p+q)/(SO(p)\times SO(q)),\\
\mathcal D^{\mathbb R}_{q,p}
 &=SO_0(p,q)/(SO(p)\times SO(q)).
\end{aligned}
\tag{AK.5}
\]
where
\[
\mathcal D^{\mathbb R}_{q,p}
=\{Z\in M_{q,p}(\mathbb R):I-Z^TZ>0\}
\tag{AK.6}
\]
and \(SO_0(p,q)\) is the identity component of the determinant-one group preserving \(\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\). Both full stabilizers in (AK.5) are connected. In graph coordinates the metrics are
\[
b^\epsilon_Z(U,V)
=\operatorname{tr}\bigl((I+\epsilon Z^TZ)^{-1}
             U^T(I+\epsilon ZZ^T)^{-1}V\bigr),
\qquad \epsilon=1,-1.
\tag{AK.7}
\]
They make the two spaces complete Riemannian symmetric spaces. The first is compact and connected; the second is bounded, convex and simply connected. Their curvature operators in graph tangent coordinates are
\[
R^\epsilon(Z,W)V
=\epsilon\bigl(ZW^TV-WZ^TV+VW^TZ-VZ^TW\bigr).
\tag{AK.8}
\]
The map forgetting orientation on the first space is a two-sheeted local isometry onto the unoriented Grassmannian. No simple-connectivity assertion about the compact quotient is implicit here.

**Proof.** Choose an oriented orthonormal basis of a plane and then an orthonormal basis of its perpendicular space so that the combined basis has the standard orientation of \(\mathbb R^{p+q}\). Gram–Schmidt here is the finite projection and normalization induction in K.1, over \(\mathbb R\); the sign of a last complement vector can be changed because \(q\geq1\). The resulting matrix lies in \(SO(p+q)\). This proves transitivity on oriented planes. A special orthogonal transformation preserving the oriented reference plane is block diagonal with its first block in \(SO(p)\); determinant one forces its second block into \(SO(q)\). Conversely every such block diagonal transformation stabilizes it. [Affine transformations G.1](affine-transformations-and-the-isometry-group.md#theorem-g-1) proves connectedness of each special orthogonal group by successive rotations in coordinate planes, including \(SO(1)=\{1\}\). Thus both this group and its stabilizer are connected. Closedness, compactness and the smooth quotient follow from [Invariant connections A.1](invariant-connections-on-homogeneous-bundles.md#theorem-a-1), [Local tools 0.1](local-tools-for-bundles-and-transport.md#0-analytic-and-linear-foundations) and [Local tools 4.1](local-tools-for-bundles-and-transport.md#4-quotients-by-a-closed-lie-subgroup).

A graph carries the orientation obtained by projecting to the oriented first summand. The real version of \(k_Z\) in (L.4) has determinant one by (AK.4) and its first block \(P_+^{-1/2}\) has positive determinant. It therefore gives an oriented graph section. Conversely, in such a chart the coordinate is \(ca^{-1}\) and the orientation condition is \(\det a>0\); both are smooth conditions. Translated sections cover all oriented planes and justify the first smooth identification in (AK.5). Forgetting orientation has exactly two preimages at each plane. On the neighborhood of graphs over that plane the two choices give two disjoint copies of the same real matrix chart, so it is a two-sheeted covering.

For the second model all the graph identities in L.2 hold over \(\mathbb R\), with \(*\) replaced by \(T\). In particular the real \(g_Z\) of (L.14) preserves the form of signature \((p,q)\), has determinant one by (AK.4), and \(s\mapsto g_{sZ}\) is a path to it from the identity. It lies in \(SO_0(p,q)\). The action
\[
g\cdot Z=(c+dZ)(a+bZ)^{-1}
\tag{AK.9}
\]
is therefore transitive. To verify both its domain and its full stabilizer within this component, a nonzero vector \((v,Zv)\) has positive norm for the preserved form. Its image cannot have first component zero. Hence \(a+bZ\) is invertible, and its graph Gram matrix is
\[
I-W^TW=(a+bZ)^{-T}(I-Z^TZ)(a+bZ)^{-1}>0.
\]
Associativity of the graph action proves the action law. Fixing zero implies \(c=0\), and the form equations then imply \(b=0\), \(a\in O(p)\), \(d\in O(q)\).

For any form-preserving block matrix, \(a^Ta-c^Tc=I\), so \(a\) is invertible. Its determinant sign is continuous and hence constant on a connected component. On the identity component it is positive. The full stabilizer there consequently has \(\det a=1\), and the total determinant gives \(\det d=1\). Conversely \(SO(p)\times SO(q)\) lies in that component by the rotation paths above. This proves the exact stabilizer, including its connectedness. The identity component is a Lie subgroup: an exponential neighborhood of a matrix Lie group is connected and contains the identity, so its component is open; its other components are translates and therefore the identity component is also closed. [Local tools 4.1](local-tools-for-bundles-and-transport.md#4-quotients-by-a-closed-lie-subgroup) supplies the quotient. The map \(Z\mapsto g_Z(SO(p)\times SO(q))\) is a smooth global inverse to the orbit map.

The domain (AK.6) is the real operator-norm unit ball, by the real spectral proof in K.1. The vector triangle inequality proves its convexity and the finite-dimensional norm bounds prove boundedness and openness, exactly as in L.2. The contraction \(Z\mapsto sZ\) proves simple connectivity.

The graph-transition derivative and the two Gram-matrix identities (L.6)–(L.8), or (L.6), (L.13), (L.15), are identities of real matrices when the action matrix and \(Z\) are real. Thus they apply in every oriented real chart. Their cancellations prove that (AK.7) agrees on overlaps and is invariant. Positivity follows from
\[
b^\epsilon_Z(U,U)
=\bigl\|(I+\epsilon ZZ^T)^{-1/2}
 U(I+\epsilon Z^TZ)^{-1/2}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.
\tag{AK.10}
\]
The metric on an oriented graph is independent of which orientation it has; it is the graph metric of [Symmetric spaces E.2](symmetric-spaces.md#example-e-2) on the underlying plane. The covering just constructed is therefore a local isometry.

For completeness and symmetry on the oriented space, the orthogonal map \(J=\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q)\) induces an involution on oriented planes by pushing forward their oriented bases. It fixes the oriented plane \(E\) and is \(Z\mapsto-Z\) in its chart. It is an isometry of (AK.7): its tangent map is \(-I\), and the two Gram matrices do not change. It is global, even when \(\det J=-1\). Equivalently on (AK.5) it is induced by the automorphism \(g\mapsto JgJ\); this preserves the acting group and its stabilizer. The identical automorphism preserves \(SO_0(p,q)\) and acts as \(Z\mapsto-Z\) on its ball. Conjugation by the transitive actions gives global point symmetries at every point, and [Symmetric spaces A.2](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-a-2) proves completeness.

Finally the odd matrices are the real versions of (L.18); differentiation of the form equations and exponentiation as in L.3 proves this Lie-algebra identification. Their brackets are exactly the real versions of (L.19). The even action is \(V\mapsto dV-Va\), the metric at zero is \(\operatorname{tr}(U^TV)\), and [Symmetric spaces B.2](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-b-2) gives \(R=-[[\,\cdot,\cdot\,],\cdot]\). This proves (AK.8) with both signs. It is the same symmetric-algebra duality, including metric scale, as D.3 wherever the effective semisimple hypothesis is applicable. The direct construction also includes the low-dimensional flat case \(p=q=1\): its compact space is the circle of oriented lines in \(\mathbb R^2\), identified with the unit circle by the unique positively oriented unit vector on each line. □

## AL. Determinant potentials and the disc

For a smooth real function \(\phi\) on a complex coordinate domain, write \(\partial_V\phi\) for its complex-linear directional derivative and \(\bar\partial_U\) for the conjugate-linear one. More explicitly, these are the combinations \((\partial_x-i\partial_y)/2\) and \((\partial_x+i\partial_y)/2\) in each coordinate, contracted with \(V\) and \(\bar U\). Thus \(\partial_V Z=V\), \(\partial_V Z^*=0\), \(\bar\partial_U Z=0\), and \(\bar\partial_U Z^*=U^*\). We call \(\phi\) a potential for the convention \(g=\operatorname{Re}h\) precisely when
\(h(U,V)=\bar\partial_U\partial_V\phi\). This fixes the normalization.

**Proposition AL.1 (The two Grassmannian potentials).** On a compact graph chart and on the noncompact matrix ball, respectively, the metrics of L.1 and L.2 have potentials
\[
\phi_\epsilon(Z)=\epsilon\log\det(I+\epsilon Z^*Z),
\qquad \epsilon=1,-1.
\tag{AL.1}
\]
For a graph transition \(W=(c+dZ)(a+bZ)^{-1}\) induced by the corresponding unitary group, let \(L=a+bZ\). On its domain,
\[
\phi_\epsilon(W)=\phi_\epsilon(Z)-\epsilon\log|\det L|^2.
\tag{AL.2}
\]
The final term has zero mixed complex Hessian. In particular (AL.1) is a global real potential on the matrix ball; in the compact case it supplies compatible local potentials. Their fundamental two-forms are \(\omega=(i/2)\partial\bar\partial\phi_\epsilon\).

**Proof.** We first prove the determinant differential used in the calculation. In the permutation expansion of \(\det(I+tC)\), the constant coefficient is one. A term linear in \(t\) must use one entry of \(tC\) and identity entries in all other positions, hence must use the identity permutation and a diagonal entry \(C_{jj}\). Thus
\(\det(I+tC)=1+t\operatorname{tr}C+O(t^2)\).
Multiplicativity of the determinant now gives, for an invertible matrix \(A\),
\[
d(\det)_A(H)=\det A\,\operatorname{tr}(A^{-1}H),
\qquad
d(\log\det)_A(H)=\operatorname{tr}(A^{-1}H)
\tag{AL.3}
\]
on the positive Hermitian cone. The second equality uses the ordinary real derivative of the logarithm of the positive determinant. The identity \(AA^{-1}=I\), differentiated, also proves
\[
d(A^{-1})_A(H)=-A^{-1}HA^{-1}.
\tag{AL.4}
\]
These formulas hold after complex-linear extension of the real directional derivatives.

Set \(P=I+\epsilon Z^*Z\) and \(Q=I+\epsilon ZZ^*\); both are positive by K.1. Since \(\epsilon^2=1\), (AL.3) gives
\[
\partial_V\phi_\epsilon=\operatorname{tr}(P^{-1}Z^*V).
\]
Applying \(\bar\partial_U\) and (AL.4), with all matrix factors in their stated order, gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\bar\partial_U\partial_V\phi_\epsilon
&=\operatorname{tr}(P^{-1}U^*V)
-\epsilon\operatorname{tr}(P^{-1}U^*ZP^{-1}Z^*V)\\
&=\operatorname{tr}\bigl(P^{-1}U^*
                      (I-\epsilon ZP^{-1}Z^*)V\bigr)\\
&=\operatorname{tr}(P^{-1}U^*Q^{-1}V).
\end{aligned}
\tag{AL.5}
\]
For the last equality, \(QZ=ZP\); multiplying \(I-\epsilon ZP^{-1}Z^*\) on the left by \(Q\) gives
\(Q-\epsilon ZPP^{-1}Z^*=I\). This proves the inverse identity and then exactly the metrics (L.3) and (L.12), including their scale. Their positivity and Kähler property were proved in L.1 and L.2; nothing about a potential is being used as a substitute for those proofs.

For clarity this last two-form assertion is also a coordinate identity. In the scalar entries \(z_j\) of \(Z\), put \(c_{j\bar k}=\partial_{z_j}\partial_{\bar z_k}\phi_\epsilon\). Then \(h(U,V)=\sum c_{j\bar k}V_j\bar U_k\). The real two-form
\[
\omega=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{j,k}c_{j\bar k}\,dz_j\wedge d\bar z_k
\]
evaluated on \(U,V\) equals \((i/2)(h(V,U)-h(U,V))=\operatorname{Im}h(U,V)=g(iU,V)\). It is closed: in its exterior derivative the coefficients of two holomorphic coordinate differentials are symmetric in their holomorphic indices, by commutation of third derivatives of the smooth potential, while their wedge product is antisymmetric. The same argument cancels the terms with two antiholomorphic differentials. This proves the formula and closedness directly in the stated convention.

Equations (L.7) and (L.13) both state \(P(W)=L^{-*}P(Z)L^{-1}\). Taking positive determinants gives
\(\det P(W)=\det P(Z)/|\det L|^2\), and real logarithms give (AL.2). To verify the zero-Hessian assertion without assuming a holomorphic logarithm, let \(f=\det L\), a nonvanishing holomorphic function on this coordinate domain. Differentiating the real function \(\log(f\bar f)\) gives
\(\partial_V\log|f|^2=(\partial_Vf)/f\).
For constant coordinate \(V\) this is holomorphic, since \(f\ne0\); its conjugate-complex derivative is therefore zero. This proves the mixed-Hessian claim. The complex chain rule introduces no extra mixed term under a holomorphic coordinate change, because \(\bar\partial_U(dW(V))=0\). Thus the local Hessians transform as the already identified Hermitian metric. □

**Proposition AL.2 (The Siegel potential with its exact scale).** For the metric (M.10) on \(\mathcal H_r\), the global potential is
\[
\psi(W)=-4\log\det Y,\qquad Y=\frac{W-\bar W}{2i}.
\tag{AL.6}
\]
For \(W'=(AW+B)(CW+D)^{-1}\) and \(N=CW+D\),
\[
\psi(W')=\psi(W)+4\log|\det N|^2.
\tag{AL.7}
\]
Under the Cayley map \(W=i(I+Z)(I-Z)^{-1}\) on the symmetric matrix ball,
\[
\psi(W(Z))
=-4\log\det(I-Z^*Z)+4\log|\det(I-Z)|^2.
\tag{AL.8}
\]
Its mixed Hessian is therefore exactly the metric (M.18).

**Proof.** Work in the complex vector space of symmetric matrices, with constant symmetric tangent vectors \(U,V\). Positivity of \(Y\) and the positive real logarithm are justified by (M.1). The coordinate derivatives are
\[
\partial_VY=\frac{V}{2i},\qquad
\bar\partial_UY=-\frac{\bar U}{2i}.
\]
Using (AL.3)–(AL.4) yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
\partial_V\psi&=2i\,\operatorname{tr}(Y^{-1}V),\\
\bar\partial_U\partial_V\psi
&=2i\,\operatorname{tr}
\left(-Y^{-1}\left(-\frac{\bar U}{2i}\right)Y^{-1}V\right)
=\operatorname{tr}(Y^{-1}\bar UY^{-1}V).
\end{aligned}
\tag{AL.9}
\]
This is precisely (M.10). Treating the tangent matrices as symmetric throughout automatically counts each off-diagonal coordinate twice, as in (N.1); no independent-coordinate factor is missing.

The domain and invertibility of \(N\) were proved in M.1. Equation (M.7) says \(Y'=N^{-*}YN^{-1}\), so taking determinants gives (AL.7). Its extra term has zero mixed Hessian by the explicit holomorphic-function calculation in AL.1. The Cayley map is a biholomorphism by M.3, and (M.20), with \(A=I-Z\) and \(P=I-Z^*Z\), says \(Y=A^{-*}PA^{-1}\). Taking determinants and logarithms gives (AL.8). Finally restricting the minus-sign Hessian (AL.5) to the complex linear subspace of symmetric matrices introduces no derivative correction: a constant symmetric tangent is also a constant tangent in the ambient matrix space. The last term of (AL.8) again has zero mixed Hessian. The result is four times that restriction, exactly (M.18). This proves both transformation laws and the normalization. □

**Proposition AL.3 (Disc translations and reflections).** For \(a\) in the unit disc \(\mathbb D\), the map
\[
f_a(z)=\frac{z-a}{1-\bar a z}
\tag{AL.10}
\]
is a biholomorphic isometry for \(g_-=|dz|^2/(1-|z|^2)^2\), with inverse
\[
f_a^{-1}(w)=\frac{w+a}{1+\bar a w}.
\tag{AL.11}
\]
The point symmetry at \(a\) is
\[
s_a(z)=f_a^{-1}(-f_a(z))
=\frac{2a-(1+|a|^2)z}{1+|a|^2-2\bar a z}.
\tag{AL.12}
\]
These statements also hold for the metric \(4g_-\). Their Gaussian curvatures are respectively \(-4\) and \(-1\).

**Proof.** The denominators of (AL.10) and (AL.11) are nonzero on the disc because \(|\bar az|<1\) and \(|\bar aw|<1\). Expanding two squared absolute values gives
\[
|1-\bar az|^2-|z-a|^2=(1-|a|^2)(1-|z|^2),
\]
and hence
\[
1-|f_a(z)|^2
=\frac{(1-|a|^2)(1-|z|^2)}{|1-\bar az|^2},
\qquad
f_a'(z)=\frac{1-|a|^2}{(1-\bar az)^2}.
\tag{AL.13}
\]
The first identity proves that \(f_a\) maps the disc into itself. Replacing \(a\) by \(-a\) proves the same for (AL.11); direct substitution shows that they are inverses. Their nonzero denominators make both maps holomorphic. Substitution of (AL.13) into the metric gives
\[
\frac{|f_a'(z)|^2}{(1-|f_a(z)|^2)^2}
=\frac1{(1-|z|^2)^2},
\]
which proves the isometry assertion. The map \(z\mapsto-z\) is also an isometry, is an involution and has derivative \(-1\) at zero. Since \(f_a(a)=0\), conjugating this map proves that \(s_a\) is an involutive isometry fixing \(a\) with derivative \(-1\). Combining the two fractions gives exactly (AL.12). Its displayed denominator is nonzero as well: \(1+|a|^2-2|a||z|>(1-|a|)^2>0\). Multiplying a metric by four preserves its isometries. The curvature values, including their normalization, were proved in L.4. □

## AM. Complex quadrics with the induced metric

We use \(g_{\mathrm{FS}}\) for the projective metric obtained from L.1 with \(p=1\). Its holomorphic sectional curvature is \(4\), by L.3. A positive constant \(\alpha\) specifies the ambient metric \(\alpha g_{\mathrm{FS}}\). The bilinear expression \(z^Tz=\sum z_j^2\) below has no complex conjugation.

**Theorem AM.1 (The quadric and the oriented two-plane model).** For \(q\geq1\), the set
\[
Q^q=\{[z]\in\mathbb {CP}^{q+1}:z^Tz=0\}
\tag{AM.1}
\]
is a smooth complex hypersurface. The map
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Phi:\mathrm{Gr}^{+}_2(\mathbb R^{q+2})&\longrightarrow Q^q,\\
\operatorname{span}^{+}(u,v)&\longmapsto[u+iv],
\end{aligned}
\tag{AM.2}
\]
where \(u,v\) is any positively oriented orthonormal basis, is a diffeomorphism. It identifies \(Q^q\) with
\[
SO(q+2)/(SO(2)\times SO(q)).
\tag{AM.3}
\]
At the reference plane, write a real graph tangent as \(X=(x,y)\in M_{q,2}(\mathbb R)\). The induced metric and complex structure are exactly
\[
b_\alpha(X,Y)=\frac{\alpha}{2}\operatorname{tr}(X^TY),
\qquad J(x,y)=(-y,x).
\tag{AM.4}
\]
Globally the metric is \(\alpha/2\) times the compact real graph metric (AK.7). The quadric is compact, simply connected and complete Hermitian symmetric. At an oriented plane \(E\), its point symmetry is induced on projective space by the real orthogonal transformation that is \(+I\) on \(E\) and \(-I\) on \(E^\perp\).

**Proof.** Let \(z=a+ib\ne0\) with \(a,b\) real. The null equation is
\[
|a|^2=|b|^2,\qquad a\cdot b=0.
\tag{AM.5}
\]
Their common length is positive, so they determine an oriented two-plane. Multiplication of \(z\) by \(c+id\ne0\) changes its real and imaginary parts to
\((ca-db,da+cb)\); the corresponding change of ordered basis has determinant \(c^2+d^2>0\). Thus the oriented plane depends only on \([z]\). Conversely rotation of a positively oriented orthonormal basis changes \(u+iv\) by a unit complex scalar. This proves that (AM.2) is well defined and is a bijection, with the inverse just described.

Here are explicit holomorphic charts on the image. For a point \([z_0]\in Q^q\), choose a representative with \(z_0^*z_0=1\). Then
\(z_0^Tz_0=0\) and \(z_0^T\bar z_0=1\). Let \(W\) be the complexification of the real perpendicular space to the plane determined by \(z_0\). The vectors \(z_0,\bar z_0\) form a Hermitian orthonormal basis of the complexification of that plane; together with \(W\) they give a direct decomposition of \(\mathbb C^{q+2}\). On the projective chart where \(z^T\bar z_0\ne0\), normalize this coefficient to one and write
\[
z=z_0+w+t\bar z_0,\qquad w\in W,\quad t\in\mathbb C.
\]
The null equation is exactly \(2t+w^Tw=0\). Thus
\[
w\longmapsto
\left[z_0+w-\frac12(w^Tw)\bar z_0\right]
\tag{AM.6}
\]
is a holomorphic parametrization of the whole part of \(Q^q\) in that chart. Its inverse is the \(W\)-component after the indicated projective normalization, hence is holomorphic. In the ambient affine coordinates \((w,t)\), the image is the graph of the polynomial \(-w^Tw/2\). It is therefore an embedded complex hypersurface of dimension \(q\). These charts cover \(Q^q\), since each chosen \([z_0]\) lies in its own chart.

The smooth local orthonormal frames for oriented graphs constructed in AK.2 make \(\Phi\) smooth. In the opposite direction choose a smooth nonzero projective representative locally, split it into its real and imaginary parts and divide both by their common positive length from (AM.5). This is a smooth oriented orthonormal frame, hence gives a smooth map to the oriented Grassmannian. The two inverse maps are smooth. They intertwine the real \(SO(q+2)\) action and its complex-linear projective action. Transitivity and the full stabilizer in (AM.3) are consequently exactly those proved in AK.2; in particular the stabilizer is connected.

We spell out the projective metric normalization. For a nonzero representative \(z\) and tangent lifts \(v,w\in\mathbb C^{q+2}\), the projective Hermitian form is
\[
h^{\mathrm{FS}}_{[z]}([v],[w])
=\frac{(z^*z)(v^*w)-(v^*z)(z^*w)}{(z^*z)^2}.
\tag{AM.7}
\]
Here \([v]\) denotes the derivative of \(t\mapsto[z+tv]\), rather than a projective point. To verify the formula from L.1, make a unitary change of coordinates taking \(z/|z|\) to the first coordinate vector. At this point the affine graph tangent is the perpendicular component of \(v\) divided by \(|z|\); L.1 gives its ordinary Hermitian inner product. Removing the components along \(z\) gives exactly (AM.7). Unitary invariance in L.1 makes this calculation valid at every point, and adding multiples of \(z\) to tangent lifts has no effect on the expression.

At \(E=\operatorname{span}^{+}(e_1,e_2)\), the oriented graph section \(k_{tX}\) of AK.2 has first two columns with derivatives \(x,y\) in \(E^\perp\). Indeed its diagonal factor \((I+t^2X^TX)^{-1/2}\) has derivative zero at zero by K.1. The derivative of \(\Phi\) is therefore represented by \(x+iy\) at \(z=e_1+ie_2\). This lift is Hermitian orthogonal to \(z\), which has squared norm two. Taking the real part in \(\alpha\) times (AM.7) proves the metric in (AM.4). Multiplication of the lift by \(i\) sends \(x+iy\) to \(-y+ix\), proving its complex-structure formula. Both metrics are invariant under the transitive group, so equality at this one plane proves the asserted global factor \(\alpha/2\).

The indicated orthogonal reflection extends complex linearly to a unitary transformation; it preserves \(z^Tz\), hence \(Q^q\), and acts holomorphically and isometrically there. It fixes \([u+iv]\) and sends every tangent lift in \(E^\perp\otimes\mathbb C\) to its negative. Thus it is a global point symmetry. The induced metric is Hermitian by (AM.7), so A.2 proves it is Kähler, and [Symmetric spaces A.2](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-a-2) proves completeness. Compactness and connectedness follow from AK.2.

For simple connectivity, the symmetric algebra is \(\mathfrak{so}(q+2)\) with even part \(\mathfrak{so}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(q)\), as in AK.2. Its even action on \(X\) is \(dX-Xa\). If it vanishes on all matrix units, \(a,d\) must be the same real scalar matrices; skew symmetry then makes them zero. Thus the action is faithful, and C.1 makes the pair effective. Its Killing form is negative definite by AC.1 for every \(q+2\geq3\), including \(\mathfrak{so}(4)\). The space is therefore of compact type, and I.2 proves that this actual compact Hermitian symmetric quotient is simply connected.

We will also use a consequence of (AM.7) and AL.1. If \(F\) is a holomorphic nonzero vector-valued local lift of a map to projective space, its pulled-back Hermitian form has potential
\[
\log(F^*F).
\tag{AM.8}
\]
To prove this, choose an entry \(F_j\ne0\). The graph coordinates of the map are the other ratios \(F_k/F_j\), and
\[
\log(F^*F)=\log|F_j|^2+
\log\left(1+\sum_{k\ne j}|F_k/F_j|^2\right).
\]
The first term has zero mixed Hessian by AL.1. The second is precisely the projective potential of AL.1 pulled back by the holomorphic graph coordinates. The complex chain rule, with its vanishing mixed derivative of a holomorphic map, proves the assertion, including the normalization in (AM.7). □

**Theorem AM.2 (Ricci scale, irreducibility and the real dual).** With the metric induced by \(\alpha g_{\mathrm{FS}}\), the quadric has
\[
\operatorname{Ric}_{Q^q}=\frac{2q}{\alpha}\,g_{Q^q}.
\tag{AM.9}
\]
It is irreducible for \(q=1\) and for \(q\geq3\). Its noncompact dual is the real matrix ball
\[
\mathcal D^{\mathbb R}_{q,2}
=SO_0(2,q)/(SO(2)\times SO(q))
\tag{AM.10}
\]
with \(\alpha/2\) times the negative-sign metric (AK.7) and the invariant complex structure whose value at zero is \(J(x,y)=(-y,x)\). This is a complete simply connected Hermitian symmetric space, and
\[
\operatorname{Ric}_{\mathcal D^{\mathbb R}_{q,2}}
=-\frac{2q}{\alpha}\,g_{\mathcal D^{\mathbb R}_{q,2}}.
\tag{AM.11}
\]

**Proof.** We compute Ricci from the already proved curvature, rather than infer the constant from a family name. On \(M_{q,p}(\mathbb R)\), use the Frobenius inner product
\(\langle U,V\rangle_F=\operatorname{tr}(U^TV)\) and its matrix-unit orthonormal basis \(E_{aj}\). By (AK.8), the trace defining compact Ricci is
\[
\sum_{a=1}^q\sum_{j=1}^p
\left\langle
E_{aj}U^TV-UE_{aj}^TV+VU^TE_{aj}-VE_{aj}^TU,\
E_{aj}\right\rangle_F.
\tag{AM.12}
\]
The four sums can be evaluated entry by entry. The first summand has selected entry \((U^TV)_{jj}\), giving \(q\operatorname{tr}(U^TV)\) after summing. The second has selected entry \(-U_{aj}V_{aj}\), giving \(-\operatorname{tr}(U^TV)\). The third has selected entry \((VU^T)_{aa}\), giving \(p\operatorname{tr}(U^TV)\). The fourth has selected entry \(-V_{aj}U_{aj}\), again giving \(-\operatorname{tr}(U^TV)\). Thus the sum is
\[
(p+q-2)\operatorname{tr}(U^TV).
\tag{AM.13}
\]
Ricci is the trace of \(T\mapsto R(T,U)V\), as in [Sectional curvature A.1](sectional-curvature-and-space-forms.md#theorem-a-1), so the computation may use any basis. In particular constant rescaling of the metric does not change this covariant Ricci tensor. Setting \(p=2\) and comparing with (AM.4) gives (AM.9) at the reference point and hence everywhere by invariance. The dual curvature is its negative by AK.2; the same trace calculation gives (AM.11) for the same tangent metric.

For comparison with the Killing normalization, we also compute its coefficient directly. On \(\mathfrak{so}(N)\), \(N\geq2\), let \(F_{ij}=E_{ij}-E_{ji}\) for \(i<j\). The Killing form \(B(A,B)=\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{ad}A\operatorname{ad}B)\) is invariant under conjugation by every orthogonal matrix, because conjugation intertwines the adjoint operators. For two distinct unordered index pairs, choose an index belonging to exactly one pair and change the sign of that coordinate. This conjugation negates exactly one of the corresponding \(F\)'s, so their Killing pairing is zero. Their ordinary trace pairing is also zero, by matrix multiplication.

For \(F_{12}\), the two-dimensional span of \(F_{1k},F_{2k}\), for each \(k>2\), is invariant under its adjoint action:
\[
[F_{12},F_{1k}]=-F_{2k},\qquad
[F_{12},F_{2k}]=F_{1k}.
\]
The square of that action is \(-I\). Its action on \(F_{12}\) and on all \(F_{k\ell}\) with \(k,\ell>2\) is zero. Hence \(B(F_{12},F_{12})=-2(N-2)\), whereas \(\operatorname{tr}(F_{12}^2)=-2\). Coordinate permutations give the same diagonal coefficient for every pair. Bilinearity and the vanishing cross pairings prove
\[
B(A,B)=(N-2)\operatorname{tr}(AB).
\tag{AM.15}
\]
In the compact odd block \(P_X=\begin{pmatrix}0&-X^T\\X&0\end{pmatrix}\), multiplication gives \(\operatorname{tr}(P_XP_Y)=-2\operatorname{tr}(X^TY)\). At \(N=q+2\), therefore, \(B(P_X,P_Y)=-2q\operatorname{tr}(X^TY)\), consistent with the Ricci calculation above.

It remains to justify the complex and global assertions for (AM.10). The full stabilizer \(SO(2)\times SO(q)\) acts by \(X\mapsto dXa^{-1}\). Put
\[
j=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix};
\qquad JX=-Xj.
\tag{AM.14}
\]
Every element of \(SO(2)\) is \(\begin{pmatrix}c&-s\\s&c\end{pmatrix}\) with \(c^2+s^2=1\), as follows from its two oriented orthonormal columns. It commutes with \(j\). Thus (AM.14) commutes with the action of the entire stabilizer, squares to \(-I\), and preserves the positive tangent metric \(\alpha\langle\,\cdot,\cdot\,\rangle_F/2\). A.3 supplies actual complex coordinates, parallelism and the complete Hermitian symmetric structure; AK.2 supplies the simply connected convex global domain. This argument does not claim that the real entries of \(X\) themselves are holomorphic coordinates away from zero.

The effective symmetric pair in either case is BDI with parameters \(2,q\), and the exact opposite-bracket realization is AK.2. AE.2 proves its irreducibility when \(q\ne2\); geometric irreducibility follows by the curvature-algebra argument in AJ.2. More explicitly a nontrivial Riemannian product would split its tangent space into nonzero subspaces invariant under all curvature operators, hence under the effective even algebra generated by the odd brackets. That contradicts the irreducible isotropy representation proved in AE.2. □

## AN. The conic and the quadric surface

**Proposition AN.1 (The conic as a scaled projective line).** The map
\[
\begin{aligned}
\nu:\mathbb {CP}^1&\longrightarrow Q^1\subset\mathbb {CP}^2,\\
[s:t]&\longmapsto[s^2-t^2:i(s^2+t^2):2st]
\end{aligned}
\tag{AN.1}
\]
is a biholomorphism. If \(Q^1\) has the metric induced by \(\alpha g_{\mathrm{FS}}\), then
\[
\nu^*g_{Q^1}=2\alpha g_{\mathrm{FS}}.
\tag{AN.2}
\]
Consequently its Gaussian curvature is \(2/\alpha\).

**Proof.** The three homogeneous quadratic coordinates in (AN.1) do not vanish simultaneously: their first coordinate minus \(i\) times their second is \(2s^2\), and their first plus \(i\) times their second is \(-2t^2\). Homogeneity gives a well-defined holomorphic map on projective space. Direct expansion gives
\[
(s^2-t^2)^2+\bigl(i(s^2+t^2)\bigr)^2+(2st)^2=0,
\]
so it maps into \(Q^1\).

For the global inverse, put \(A=z_1-iz_2\), \(B=z_1+iz_2\), \(C=z_3\). The null equation is \(AB=-C^2\), and \(A,B\) cannot both be zero. On \(A\ne0\), set \([s:t]=[1:C/A]\). In these coordinates the image of (AN.1) has \((A,B,C)=(2,-2(t/s)^2,2(t/s))\); after multiplication by \(A/2\) this is the prescribed triple. On \(B\ne0\), set \([s:t]=[-C/B:1]\); the corresponding image triple is \((2(s/t)^2,-2,2(s/t))\), which becomes the prescribed one after multiplication by \(-B/2\). On the overlap the two ratios agree because \(AB=-C^2\). These are holomorphic inverse maps in projective charts. Thus \(\nu\) is a biholomorphism, including the points at either chart boundary.

The squared norm of the polynomial lift is
\[
|s^2-t^2|^2+|s^2+t^2|^2+4|st|^2
=2(|s|^2+|t|^2)^2.
\tag{AN.3}
\]
On the chart \(s\ne0\), take \(s=1\). The pullback potential (AM.8) for the ambient scale \(\alpha\) is
\(\alpha\log2+2\alpha\log(1+|t|^2)\).
Its mixed Hessian is \(2\alpha\) times the projective-line metric by AL.1. The same calculation on \(t=1\) covers the other chart, so it proves the global equality (AN.2). L.4 gives curvature \(4\) for \(g_{\mathrm{FS}}\) on \(\mathbb {CP}^1\) and proves that rescaling divides sectional curvature by the scale. Hence the curvature is \(4/(2\alpha)=2/\alpha\), agreeing with (AM.9) in real dimension two. □

**Proposition AN.2 (The quadric surface as an exact metric product).** The complex-linear map
\[
T:\mathbb C^4\longrightarrow M_2(\mathbb C),\qquad
T(z)=\frac1{\sqrt2}
\begin{pmatrix}
z_1+iz_2&z_3+iz_4\\
-z_3+iz_4&z_1-iz_2
\end{pmatrix}
\tag{AN.4}
\]
is unitary for the standard Hermitian and Hilbert–Schmidt forms. Its projectivization identifies \(Q^2\) with the nonzero rank-one matrices modulo scalar. The Segre map
\[
\begin{aligned}
\sigma:\mathbb {CP}^1\times\mathbb {CP}^1
&\longrightarrow\mathbb P(M_2(\mathbb C)),\\
([u],[v])&\longmapsto[uv^T]
\end{aligned}
\tag{AN.5}
\]
therefore gives a biholomorphism \(F=\mathbb P(T)^{-1}\circ\sigma\) onto \(Q^2\). At ambient scale \(\alpha\) it satisfies
\[
F^*g_{Q^2}=\alpha g_{\mathrm{FS}}\oplus\alpha g_{\mathrm{FS}}.
\tag{AN.6}
\]
Each projective-line factor has Gaussian curvature \(4/\alpha\); planes tangent to different factors have sectional curvature zero.

**Proof.** Expanding the four absolute squares of the entries of (AN.4), the mixed terms in each pair cancel, yielding
\[
\|T(z)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\sum_{j=1}^4|z_j|^2.
\]
The polarization identity proves preservation of the Hermitian form: its real part is obtained by applying this squared-norm equality to \(z+w,z-w\), and its imaginary part by applying it to \(z+iw,z-iw\). The inverse linear equations are
\[
\begin{aligned}
z_1&=(M_{11}+M_{22})/\sqrt2,&
z_2&=(M_{11}-M_{22})/(i\sqrt2),\\
z_3&=(M_{12}-M_{21})/\sqrt2,&
z_4&=(M_{12}+M_{21})/(i\sqrt2).
\end{aligned}
\tag{AN.7}
\]
Thus \(T\) is a unitary isomorphism. Direct multiplication gives
\[
\det T(z)=\frac12(z_1^2+z_2^2+z_3^2+z_4^2).
\tag{AN.8}
\]
A nonzero \(2\)-by-\(2\) matrix has determinant zero exactly when it has rank one: a nonzero column spans every column, by the vanishing determinant, and conversely dependent columns have zero determinant. This proves the image assertion for \(Q^2\).

The matrix \(uv^T\) is nonzero when \(u,v\ne0\); rescaling either vector merely rescales the matrix, so (AN.5) is well defined and holomorphic in projective charts. Every rank-one matrix has this form: choose a nonzero column \(u\), and write each column as a multiple of it, with those multiples comprising \(v^T\). The two projective vectors are uniquely determined. Indeed the image column line determines \([u]\), and then any nonzero row determines \([v]\). The inverse is holomorphic, not only set-theoretic: on a chart \(M_{ij}\ne0\), use the \(j\)-th column for \(u\) and set \(v_\ell=M_{i\ell}/M_{ij}\). These formulas give \(M=uv^T\) and have nonvanishing denominators on the stated chart. Their projective values agree on overlaps by uniqueness. This proves the global biholomorphism.

The projective transformation induced by \(T\) preserves \(g_{\mathrm{FS}}\), by its unitary invariance proved in L.1 or by (AM.7). It therefore remains to compute the pullback under \(\sigma\). On any product of standard projective charts choose holomorphic vector lifts \(u,v\) with one coordinate of each equal to one. The elementary identity
\[
\|uv^T\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
=\left(\sum_i|u_i|^2\right)\left(\sum_j|v_j|^2\right)
\tag{AN.9}
\]
and the pullback potential formula (AM.8) give
\[
\alpha\log\|uv^T\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
=\alpha\log\|u\|^2+\alpha\log\|v\|^2.
\]
The first term depends only on the first factor and the second only on the second. Their mixed Hessian has zero cross blocks and has precisely \(\alpha h_{\mathrm{FS}}\) on each diagonal block, by AL.1. This proves (AN.6) on a covering family of charts and hence globally.

Each factor has curvature \(4/\alpha\) by L.4. The Koszul formula in [Symmetric spaces A.1](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-a-1) shows that the product metric has the product connection: vector fields lifted from different factors commute, have zero cross inner products, and their within-factor inner products are constant in the other factor's direction. Thus all cross covariant derivatives vanish and the connection in one factor is its own Levi-Civita connection. It follows that mixed curvature is zero. This gives the last assertion and proves, at the specified metric scale, the reducibility of \(Q^2\). □

## AO. Complex totally geodesic subspaces

**Theorem AO.1 (The complex tangent criterion).** Let \(f:N\to M\) be a connected totally geodesic immersed submanifold of a Hermitian symmetric space. Give \(N\) its induced Riemannian metric. Its local immersed branches are complex submanifolds if and only if
\[
J_{f(x)}\,df_x(T_xN)=df_x(T_xN)
\tag{AO.1}
\]
at one point \(x\in N\). In that case (AO.1) holds at every point, and the branches give \(N\) a complex atlas for which \(f\) is a holomorphic immersion. Completeness is not required for this criterion. In particular, a Lie triple system \(V\subseteq T_oM\) has a complete complex totally geodesic immersed image exactly when \(J_oV=V\).

**Proof.** The complex structure \(J\) of the ambient space is parallel by A.1–A.2. Total geodesy is equivalent to preservation of the tangent spaces by ambient parallel transport, by [Submanifolds G.1](submanifolds-and-hypersurfaces.md#proposition-g-1), since the Levi-Civita connection has zero torsion. If \(P\) is transport along any curve in \(N\), then \(PJ=JP\) and \(P\) identifies its endpoint tangent spaces. Thus invariance at one endpoint implies invariance at the other. A connected manifold is joined by finite geodesic polygons, by [Hopf–Rinow E.1](completeness-and-the-hopf-rinow-theorem.md#lemma-e-1). This proves (AO.1) everywhere.

We justify complex coordinates on each branch explicitly. The homogeneous exponential charts of [Homogeneous spaces G.3](homogeneous-spaces-and-invariant-connections.md#lemma-g-3) are real analytic, and the invariant complex charts constructed in [Homogeneous spaces G.4](homogeneous-spaces-and-invariant-connections.md#theorem-g-4) are compatible with that real-analytic atlas. These apply to the Hermitian symmetric presentation of A.3; [Symmetric spaces B.3](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-b-3) supplies such a presentation for a given Hermitian symmetric space, by its transvections, which preserve \(J\). In a symmetric presentation, [Symmetric spaces B.2](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-b-2) identifies the homogeneous curve \(\exp(tX)H\) with the Riemannian geodesic of initial vector \(X\). The local uniqueness argument in [Symmetric spaces C.3](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-c-3) consequently identifies the branch through \(f(x)\) with
\[
v\longmapsto\exp_{f(x)}(v),\qquad
v\in df_x(T_xN)\text{ near }0.
\tag{AO.2}
\]
It is an analytic embedding in the homogeneous charts and hence in the compatible complex charts. Thus the branch is real analytic, rather than merely smooth.

Write its real dimension as \(2k\), which is even by (AO.1). In an ambient complex chart at the point, choose a complex-linear projection to \(\mathbb C^k\) that is an isomorphism on its complex tangent space. Such a projection is obtained by extending a complex basis of that tangent space to one of the ambient vector space. The analytic inverse theorem of [Curvature and holonomy I.1](curvature-and-holonomy-groups.md#lemma-i-1) makes the branch, locally, an analytic graph
\[
w\longmapsto (w,F(w)),\qquad w\in\mathbb C^k.
\]
Its tangent vectors have the form \((u,dF_wu)\). Invariance under multiplication by \(i\) says that \((iu,i\,dF_wu)\) is again a tangent vector; comparison of its first component forces
\[
dF_w(iu)=i\,dF_wu.
\tag{AO.3}
\]
The analytic Cauchy–Riemann proof in [Homogeneous spaces G.1](homogeneous-spaces-and-invariant-connections.md#lemma-g-1) now makes \(F\) holomorphic. These graph coordinates are therefore genuine complex submanifold coordinates. The change between two branch coordinates is a restriction of a holomorphic ambient coordinate change followed by a complex-linear projection, and is holomorphic; its inverse is obtained in the same way. Pulling the coordinates back to the local embedded branches of the immersion gives the claimed atlas on \(N\).

Conversely the derivative of a holomorphic immersion is complex linear, so its image tangent space is invariant under \(J\). This proves necessity as well. For a Lie triple system, [Symmetric spaces C.4](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-c-4) supplies the connected complete immersed image, with its intrinsic orbit topology; its induced connection is the Levi-Civita connection by the metric restriction and zero torsion. Applying the criterion just proved gives the last assertion. For a zero-dimensional connected \(N\), the space is a point and the same conclusion holds with \(\mathbb C^0\). □

**Theorem AO.2 (All complete complex subspaces in rank one).** In projective space \(\mathbb {CP}^m\) with any positive multiple of the metric of L.1, the images of connected complete complex totally geodesic immersions are exactly its projective linear subspaces \(\mathbb P(E)\), where \(E\subseteq\mathbb C^{m+1}\) is a nonzero complex linear subspace. Each such immersion is an embedding onto its image.

In the complex hyperbolic ball \(\mathcal D_{m,1}\) of L.2, with any positive multiple of its metric, the corresponding images are exactly the holomorphic isometric translates of
\[
\{(z_1,\ldots,z_k,0,\ldots,0):
 |z_1|^2+\cdots+|z_k|^2<1\},
\qquad 0\leq k\leq m.
\tag{AO.4}
\]
Again each complete immersion is an embedding onto that image. The case \(k=0\) means a point. In the positive-line interpretation of AK.1, these are the positive lines in complex linear subspaces of signature \((1,k)\).

**Proof.** First fix a point \(o\) and a complex \(k\)-dimensional tangent subspace \(V\). By the transitive holomorphic isometries of L.1–L.2 we may put \(o\) at the reference line or at the origin of the ball. Its tangent space is then \(\mathbb C^m\). A unitary transformation sends \(V\) to its first \(k\) coordinates: choose an orthonormal basis of \(V\), extend it by orthonormal vectors in its perpendicular complement, and use the resulting unitary matrix. This is an allowed stabilizer action, by L.1–L.2. Therefore it suffices to construct and identify the coordinate subspace with that tangent.

For projective space let
\[
E=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb C}(e_0,e_1,\ldots,e_k).
\]
The inclusion \(\mathbb P(E)\subseteq\mathbb {CP}^m\) is holomorphic and embedded in the usual projective charts. Its induced metric is the same scaled projective metric in dimension \(k\): insert a graph vector with its last \(m-k\) entries zero in (L.3). The inverse matrices restrict to the corresponding first blocks and the zero blocks contribute nothing to the trace. It is complete by L.1.

It is totally geodesic as follows. The unitary transformation equal to \(+I\) on \(E\) and \(-I\) on \(E^\perp\) induces an ambient isometry fixing \(\mathbb P(E)\) pointwise and acting as the identity on its tangent vectors. Its projective fixed points are exactly \(\mathbb P(E)\) and, when \(E^\perp\ne0\), \(\mathbb P(E^\perp)\). Indeed a vector with nonzero components in both eigenspaces cannot be multiplied by a single scalar to equal its image. An ambient geodesic initially tangent to \(\mathbb P(E)\) is fixed by the isometry, by uniqueness of the geodesic equation, [Geodesics A.1](geodesics-normal-coordinates-and-curvature.md#theorem-a-1). Locally at its starting point it therefore lies in \(\mathbb P(E)\), because the other fixed subspace is closed and disjoint from that point. This proves total geodesy; when \(E\) is the whole space it is immediate.

The tangent-generated algebra is also explicit. For \(k\geq1\), its compact odd matrices occupy only the \((k+1)\)-coordinate block \(E\); their even brackets and triple brackets stay in that block. AK.1, applied with \(p=1,q=k\), proves that they generate exactly \(\mathfrak{su}(k+1)\): the odd entries, their even off-diagonal brackets and diagonal differences span that algebra. The corresponding block subgroup has projective orbit \(\mathbb P(E)\), by the same transitivity proof. The noncompact block is \(\mathfrak{su}(1,k)\), with its opposite odd bracket proved in L.3 and its full orbit given by AK.1. For \(k=0\), the tangent-generated algebra is zero and the orbit is a point.

For the ball, the linear unitary isometry that keeps the first \(k\) coordinates and negates the others has fixed set exactly (AO.4). The same geodesic uniqueness proves total geodesy. Restriction of (L.12) to those coordinates gives exactly the dimension-\(k\) ball metric, which is complete by L.2. The inclusion is holomorphic and embedded. In both settings this constructs a complete subspace \(P\) through \(o\) with tangent \(V\); the limiting cases \(k=0,m\) are included.

Now let \(f:N\to M\) be a connected complete totally geodesic immersion with a complex tangent space at \(x\), and put \(o=f(x)\), \(V=df_x(T_xN)\). AO.1 makes it a complex immersion. For every \(v\in T_xN\), total geodesy and the geodesic uniqueness theorem give
\[
f(\exp_x^N v)=\exp_o^M(df_xv).
\tag{AO.5}
\]
Both sides are defined at every \(v\). By [Hopf–Rinow B.2](completeness-and-the-hopf-rinow-theorem.md#theorem-b-2), the exponential at \(x\) is onto \(N\), so
\[
f(N)=\exp_o^M(V).
\tag{AO.6}
\]
Apply exactly the same reasoning to the complete coordinate subspace \(P\) constructed above. Its exponential is onto and its geodesics are ambient geodesics, hence \(P=\exp_o^M(V)\). Thus \(f(N)=P\), proving the claimed classification of images.

There is no hidden multiple covering in this statement. Regarding \(f\) as a map \(N\to P\), its derivative is injective and the two dimensions agree, so it is a local diffeomorphism. The induced metric makes it a local isometry, hence affine by the Koszul uniqueness in [Symmetric spaces A.1](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-a-1). [Hopf–Rinow E.2](completeness-and-the-hopf-rinow-theorem.md#theorem-e-2) makes it a covering. The projective subspace is simply connected by AK.1, and the ball is simply connected by the convex contraction in L.2; the zero-dimensional case is a point. [Flat connections D.3](flat-connections-and-infinitesimal-holonomy.md#theorem-d-3) says that a connected covering of a simply connected space is a diffeomorphism. This proves that \(f\), as a map into the ambient manifold, is an embedding onto \(P\).

Finally, in the positive-line model, the coordinate subball consists of positive lines in \(\mathbb C e_0\oplus\mathbb C^k\), whose restricted form has signature \((1,k)\). An isometry preserves the ambient Hermitian form, so its translates have the asserted description. Conversely in any such subspace choose a positive unit vector. By the transitivity in L.2, move its line to the first coordinate line; a unit scalar adjusts the chosen vector itself. Its ambient orthogonal complement is now the negative definite last \(m\) coordinates. Removing the first component of vectors in the given subspace consequently leaves a negative definite \(k\)-space there. Gram–Schmidt for the negative of the restricted form gives an orthonormal negative basis. Extend it within the ambient negative perpendicular complement. The resulting form-preserving basis change sends the subspace to the coordinate model and acts by the holomorphic isometries of L.2. This proves the final equivalence. □

## AP. The compact classical quotient models

Use the compact quaternionic group
\(\mathrm{Sp}(n)=\{Q\in M_n(\mathbb H):Q^\dagger Q=I\}\).
Its complex realization is (AC.7). Write
\[
J_n=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_n\\-I_n&0\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
S_{p,q}=\operatorname{diag}(I_p,-I_q).
\tag{AP.1}
\]
Conjugation by a displayed matrix is denoted \(\operatorname{Ad}\). In the CI row below, \(iI_n\) is a quaternionic matrix. In the DIII row, \(J_n\) is real.

**Theorem AP.1 (Actual quotients and their covering interpretation).** The seven compact classical families in AE.2 have the following connected compact homogeneous models, with the indicated group involutions:

| Family | Group \(G\) | Connected subgroup \(H\) | Involution \(\sigma\) on \(G\) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AI, \(n\geq2\) | \(SU(n)\) | \(SO(n)\) | \(g\mapsto\bar g\) |
| AII, \(n\geq2\) | \(SU(2n)\) | \(\mathrm{Sp}(n)\) in (AC.7) | \(g\mapsto J_n\bar gJ_n^{-1}\) |
| AIII, \(p,q\geq1\) | \(SU(p+q)\) | \(S(U(p)\times U(q))\) | \(\operatorname{Ad}S_{p,q}\) |
| BDI, \(p,q\geq1\) | \(SO(p+q)\) | \(SO(p)\times SO(q)\) | \(\operatorname{Ad}S_{p,q}\) |
| CI, \(n\geq1\) | \(\mathrm{Sp}(n)\) | \(U(n)\), with complex entries | \(\operatorname{Ad}(iI_n)\) |
| CII, \(p,q\geq1\) | \(\mathrm{Sp}(p+q)\) | \(\mathrm{Sp}(p)\times\mathrm{Sp}(q)\) | \(\operatorname{Ad}S_{p,q}\) |
| DIII, \(n\geq3\) | \(SO(2n)\) | \(U(n)\), acting on \(\mathbb R^{2n}\) | \(\operatorname{Ad}J_n\) |

In every row, \(H=(G^\sigma)^0\). In all rows except BDI it is the full fixed group. These quotients are smooth complete Riemannian symmetric spaces and realize exactly the compact algebraic pairs and dimensions of AE.2. Their simply connected Riemannian covers are the corresponding spaces in AJ.1–AJ.2. Thus the table specifies actual models and a precise covering operation, without assuming that an arbitrary stabilizer with the same Lie algebra gives the same quotient.

The AIII and BDI models are respectively the complex Grassmannians of AK.1 and the oriented real Grassmannians of AK.2. CI and DIII carry invariant complex structures for their full displayed stabilizers, are Hermitian symmetric and are themselves simply connected. On each irreducible row, choosing a positive multiple of the tangent metric gives every invariant metric scale in the classification. BDI at \((1,1)\) is the flat circle with simply connected cover a line; BDI at \((2,2)\) has the two rank-one factors already identified in AN.2.

**Proof.** [De Rham decomposition Y.2](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#theorem-y-2) proves that all displayed acting groups are compact connected embedded matrix Lie groups, and proves connectedness of \(SO(n),U(n),SU(n)\) and \(\mathrm{Sp}(n)\) in every size used here. [De Rham decomposition Y.3](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#theorem-y-3) and AC.2 identify the compact complex symplectic realization with \(\mathrm{Sp}(n)\) and prove its inclusion in \(SU(2n)\). Products of the connected subgroups are connected by taking their paths componentwise. The AIII stabilizer's connectedness was separately proved in AK.1, including its determinant constraint.

We check the fixed subgroups at group level. For AI, a real matrix in \(SU(n)\) is exactly a real orthogonal matrix of determinant one. For AII, the fixed equation is
\[
gJ_n=J_n\bar g.
\tag{AP.2}
\]
By AC.2 its solutions are precisely matrices of the form (AC.7); unitarity is exactly \(Q^\dagger Q=I\). Conversely all such matrices have complex determinant one by [De Rham decomposition Y.3](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#theorem-y-3), so there is no additional determinant restriction. This gives the full \(\mathrm{Sp}(n)\).

For AIII, commuting with \(S_{p,q}\) forces the off-diagonal blocks to vanish; unitarity and determinant one give exactly \(S(U(p)\times U(q))\). For BDI the same computation gives the full fixed group
\[
S(O(p)\times O(q))
=\{(a,d):\det a\,\det d=1\}.
\tag{AP.3}
\]
The signs of the two determinants cannot change along a path. Its component containing the identity is exactly \(SO(p)\times SO(q)\), since that product is connected. Thus BDI uses the connected component, a genuine distinction from the other rows.

For CI, an entry of a quaternionic matrix commutes with \(i\) exactly when it is complex. Indeed, in \(a+b\mathbf j\), the relation \(\mathbf j i=-i\mathbf j\) makes commutation equivalent to \(b=0\). The fixed unitary matrices are consequently exactly \(U(n)\). For CII, the eigenspaces of \(S_{p,q}\) force block diagonal quaternionic matrices, with independent compact symplectic blocks.

For DIII, commuting with \(J_n\) means being complex linear when \(J_n\) is the complex structure on \(\mathbb R^{2n}\). A real orthogonal map commuting with it preserves the associated Hermitian form: both the real scalar product and the form \((v,w)\mapsto\langle J_nv,w\rangle\) are preserved. Conversely a unitary map preserves that real scalar product. Such a map has real determinant \(+1\), since \(U(n)\) is connected and contains the identity, by [De Rham decomposition Y.2](holonomy-and-the-de-rham-decomposition-theorem.md#theorem-y-2). Therefore intersection with \(SO(2n)\) removes no element and the full fixed group is \(U(n)\).

All displayed automorphisms square to the identity. This is immediate for conjugation and the sign matrices. In AII, \(J_n\) is real and \(J_n^2=-I\), so applying the formula twice gives conjugation by \(-I\), hence the identity. In CI and DIII the conjugating matrix squares to the central scalar \(-I\), with the same consequence. The formulas preserve each group by their defining unitary, orthogonal or quaternionic equations. Their fixed groups and connected components are closed, and hence the displayed \(H\)'s are closed subgroups. [Symmetric spaces B.1](symmetric-spaces.md#lemma-b-1) now supplies the smooth quotients and their tangent eigenspaces.

Differentiating the displayed group formulas gives exactly the algebra involutions in AD.1–AD.3. Those complete calculations identify each fixed algebra, odd space, real dual and dimension; no pair is inferred from its label. To construct a positive invariant metric explicitly, use the faithful complex representation \(\rho\) of the acting group, with real matrices regarded as complex and quaternionic matrices represented by (AC.7). On its odd algebra put
\[
b(X,Y)=-\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}
                  \bigl(\rho(X)\rho(Y)\bigr).
\tag{AP.4}
\]
Its matrices are skew-Hermitian, so
\[
b(X,X)=\operatorname{tr}(\rho(X)^*\rho(X))>0
\quad\text{for }X\ne0.
\]
The last trace is the sum of squared absolute values of the entries, and faithfulness makes it strictly positive. Cyclicity of trace proves invariance under the entire \(H\). [Symmetric spaces B.2](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-b-2) gives its complete Levi-Civita connection, global point symmetries and curvature \(-[[X,Y],Z]\). Compactness and connectedness follow from those of \(G\) and continuity and surjectivity of the quotient map. AIII and BDI are the already proved actual identifications AK.1–AK.2.

For CI the compact odd matrices in (AC.7) have the form
\[
X_B=\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\-\bar B&0\end{pmatrix},
\qquad B^T=B,
\tag{AP.5}
\]
and the full stabilizer is \(\operatorname{diag}(U,\bar U)\).
Its action sends \(B\) to \(UBU^T\); thus \(B\mapsto iB\) commutes with the whole stabilizer, squares to \(-I\) and preserves (AP.4). A.3 gives the invariant complex coordinates and complete Hermitian symmetric structure.

For DIII the odd real matrices satisfy \(XJ_n=-J_nX\). The map \(X\mapsto J_nX\) preserves that space and its skew symmetry:
\[
(J_nX)^T=XJ_n=-J_nX.
\]
It squares to \(-I\). It commutes with conjugation by every element of \(H\), since these commute with \(J_n\). Orthogonality of \(J_n\), or direct substitution in the trace form, shows that it preserves (AP.4). A.3 again applies. The effective algebras in these ranges are compact semisimple by AC.1 and AE.2. Hence I.2 proves simple connectivity of these actual Hermitian quotients. The same property for AIII was proved in AK.1.

For the covering assertion in every row, take the smooth universal cover supplied by [Flat connections D.2](flat-connections-and-infinitesimal-holonomy.md#theorem-d-2) and pull back the metric. The covering is a local isometry, so its pulled-back connection is Levi-Civita by [Symmetric spaces A.1](symmetric-spaces.md#theorem-a-1). [Hopf–Rinow E.3](completeness-and-the-hopf-rinow-theorem.md#theorem-e-3) lifts geodesic completeness from the quotient to the cover. Curvature remains parallel and has the same value on the identified tangent space. It is therefore exactly the simply connected model of that metric pair by AJ.1, or equivalently by the metric-pair uniqueness used in AJ.2. This proves the precise global interpretation of the table. In each irreducible nonflat case, D.1 identifies every invariant positive form with a positive multiple of the Killing form; (AP.4) supplies one such form. Its positive multiples give all scales.

Finally for BDI \((1,1)\), \(G=SO(2)\) and \(H\) is trivial. Its odd algebra is abelian, so the connection is flat. The circle and its line cover were explicitly treated in G.1. For \((2,2)\), AM.1 identifies the quotient with the quadric surface and AN.2 gives its global product at the specified metric normalization; its underlying algebraic splitting is AE.2. This also records the boundary cases that cannot be read as irreducible nonflat rows. □

## AQ. The compact Siegel dual at the transferred scale

**Theorem AQ.1 (The compact CI space and the normalized dual).** The compact quotient \(\mathrm{Sp}(r)/U(r)\), \(r\geq1\), of AP.1 is the compact Hermitian symmetric dual of the actual Siegel space \(\mathcal H_r\) of M.1–M.3. In the compact odd coordinates \(X_B\) of (AP.5), its complex structure and the metric transferred from (M.10) are
\[
JB=iB,\qquad
b_c(X_B,X_D)=4\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(B^*D),
\qquad B^T=B,\quad D^T=D.
\tag{AQ.1}
\]
Both spaces are simply connected. The identification of the noncompact dual preserves the full tangent metric and complex structure, not only the real Lie algebra. For \(r=1\), the compact space is a projective line with metric \(4g_{\mathrm{FS}}\) and Gaussian curvature \(1\); its dual is the curvature-\(-1\) disc or upper half-plane of L.4.

**Proof.** The full compact stabilizer and its action \(B\mapsto UBU^T\) were proved in AP.1. This action is complex linear and preserves the Hermitian trace form: unitarity gives \(\bar U U^T=I\), and cyclicity then gives
\[
\operatorname{tr}((UBU^T)^*(UDU^T))
=\operatorname{tr}(B^*D).
\]
Thus (AQ.1) defines a positive full-stabilizer invariant Hermitian form. A.3 constructs its complex coordinates, parallel complex structure and complete symmetric metric; I.2, as in AP.1, proves simple connectivity.

We exhibit the real dual and its tangent identification. On the real symplectic group, the map \(g\mapsto(g^T)^{-1}\) is an involutive automorphism. In fact the equation \(g^T\Omega g=\Omega\) gives \((g^T)^{-1}=\Omega g\Omega^{-1}\); conjugation by the symplectic matrix \(\Omega\) preserves the group and squares to conjugation by \(-I\). Its fixed points are the orthogonal symplectic matrices. Orthogonality reduces the symplectic equation to commutation with \(\Omega\), which forces the blocks \(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\-b&a\end{pmatrix}\); the remaining orthogonality equations are exactly (M.9). Thus its full fixed group is precisely the \(U(r)\) of M.4. Its differential is \(L\mapsto-L^T\).

Put
\[
C=\frac1{\sqrt2}
\begin{pmatrix}I&-iI\\ I&iI\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{AQ.2}
\]
Its conjugate transpose is its inverse, by multiplication. A general real symplectic Lie-algebra matrix is
\[
L=\begin{pmatrix}A&F\\G&-A^T\end{pmatrix},
\qquad F^T=F,\quad G^T=G,
\tag{AQ.3}
\]
as follows by differentiating \(g^T\Omega g=\Omega\) from M.1. Block multiplication gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
CLC^{-1}&=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\\bar Q&\bar P\end{pmatrix},\\
P&=\frac{A-A^T+i(F-G)}2,\\
Q&=\frac{A+A^T-i(F+G)}2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{AQ.4}
\]
Here \(P^*=-P\) and \(Q^T=Q\). Conversely such \(P,Q\) recover real matrices in (AQ.3): the real and imaginary parts determine \(A-A^T,F-G,A+A^T,F+G\), and addition and subtraction give \(A,F,G\). Thus (AQ.4) identifies the entire real symplectic algebra with these blocks.

The compact symplectic algebra is
\[
\left\{
\begin{pmatrix}P&B\\-\bar B&\bar P\end{pmatrix}:
P^*=-P,\ B^T=B\right\},
\tag{AQ.5}
\]
by AC.2. Its even blocks are exactly the \(P\)-blocks in (AQ.4). Replace its odd matrix \(X_B\) by
\[
Y_B=\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\\bar B&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The even-odd bracket sends \(B\) to \(PB-B\bar P\) on both sides. Multiplication of two odd blocks shows that
\[
[Y_B,Y_D]=-[X_B,X_D].
\tag{AQ.6}
\]
The even-even bracket is unchanged. This proves the precise opposite-odd-bracket duality of D.3, with the same odd coordinate \(B\).

In the real coordinates (AQ.3), an odd matrix is
\[
L_{X,Y}=\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\Y&-X\end{pmatrix},
\qquad X^T=X,\quad Y^T=Y.
\tag{AQ.7}
\]
Formula (AQ.4) identifies its odd coordinate as \(B=X-iY\). Differentiate the fractional action of M.1 at \(W=iI\). Its tangent is
\[
\delta W=2(Y+iX)=2iB.
\tag{AQ.8}
\]
Indeed the derivative of \((aW+b)(cW+d)^{-1}\) at the identity is \(a'W+b'-W(c'W+d')\); substitution from (AQ.7) yields (AQ.8).

At \(W=iI\), formula (M.10) is the ordinary Hermitian trace form on symmetric tangent matrices. Substitution of \(\delta W=2iB\) and \(2iD\) therefore gives exactly \(4\operatorname{tr}(B^*D)\), whose real part is (AQ.1). The derivative at \(iI\) of the Cayley coordinate \(Z=(W-iI)(W+iI)^{-1}\) is \(\delta Z=\delta W/(2i)=B\). Thus multiplication by \(i\) in the bounded holomorphic coordinate is exactly \(B\mapsto iB\), including its sign. This proves both the complex and metric normalization.

For the full stabilizer, a real matrix of M.4 is
\(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\-b&a\end{pmatrix}\).
Conjugating it by (AQ.2) gives
\(\operatorname{diag}(a+ib,a-ib)\), and \(a+ib\) ranges over all of \(U(r)\). Its action on odd coordinates is therefore exactly \(B\mapsto UBU^T\), the compact stabilizer action. M.1 proves that the actual real group is connected and that this is its full stabilizer; M.2 proves the complete invariant Siegel metric. The domain is convex, hence simply connected by M.1. Its effective pair is precisely (AQ.4), so the existence and uniqueness theorem AJ.1 identifies it with the simply connected noncompact dual, with the metric and complex structure just checked. No covering ambiguity remains.

When \(r=1\), (AC.7) identifies \(\mathrm{Sp}(1)\) with \(SU(2)\): its matrices have the form
\(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\-\bar b&\bar a\end{pmatrix}\),
with \(|a|^2+|b|^2=1\), and every determinant-one unitary \(2\)-by-\(2\) matrix has this form by its orthonormal columns. The stabilizer of the second coordinate line is exactly the diagonal \(U(1)\). Its orbit map therefore gives the projective line, by AK.1. The derivative of \(X_B\) on that line is the affine graph coordinate \(B\), so it preserves the complex structure in (AQ.1). At the origin its metric is \(4|dB|^2\), hence by transitivity it is \(4g_{\mathrm{FS}}\) everywhere. L.4 gives its curvature \(1\) and the corresponding dual curvature \(-1\).

More generally, the AI \(n=2\), AIII \(p=q=1\), and CI \(r=1\) algebraic coincidences proved in AE.2 identify their simply connected metric realizations by AJ.1, after the positive scale is fixed. The projective-line and disc models above supply those realizations; their round-sphere interpretation is the space-form classification in [Sectional curvature D.2](sectional-curvature-and-space-forms.md#theorem-d-2). Thus the small-rank repetitions in the classical table are global metric statements with specified scales. □

## Further reading

- J.-H. Eschenburg, *Lecture Notes on Symmetric Spaces*, [freely accessible author-hosted notes](https://myweb.rz.uni-augsburg.de/~eschenbu/symspace.pdf), §1 Examples 6–8 and §§4–8, on Grassmannians, classical quotient models, totally geodesic subspaces, symmetric presentations, Lie triple systems, simply connected realizations, Killing forms, curvature signs and duality.

- Eckhard Meinrenken, *Lie groups and Lie algebras (Winter 2026)*, [freely accessible University of Toronto lecture notes](https://www.math.toronto.edu/mein/teaching/LectureNotes/lie.pdf), §8.5 and §9.2, on bi-invariant metrics, maximal tori and compact-group structure.

- A. Iozzi and Y. Krifka, *Symmetric Spaces*, Spring 2021, [Solution Exercise Sheet 5](https://metaphor.ethz.ch/x/2021/fs/401-3226-00L/ex/sol5.pdf), Exercise 3, on the symplectic action and the Siegel upper half-space.
- Ngaiming Mok, *Complex Geometry on Bounded Symmetric Domains, I. Metric rigidity*, 21 August 2006, [author-hosted lecture slides](https://hkumath.hku.hk/~nmok/I.pdf), page 2, for the rectangular matrix-ball model.

- Pavel Etingof, *Lie Groups and Lie Algebras*, MIT 18.755, Spring 2024, [freely accessible MIT OpenCourseWare notes](https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-755-lie-groups-and-lie-algebras-ii-spring-2024/mit18_755_s24_lec_full.pdf), §§6.2–6.3, §11.4, §§13.1–13.2, §14.2, §§19.1–19.4, §§20.1–20.3, §21.4, §§22.1–22.2, §§23.1–23.2, §23.8, §§24.1–24.2, §40.1, §41.1, §§41.3–41.4 and §§42.1–42.2, on rank-one representations, ordered words, free Lie algebras, Cartan subalgebras, roots, finite diagrams, the Serre presentation, compact real forms, classical matrix algebras, involutions, exceptional fixed algebras and the E6 diagram-reversal cases.

- Sebastian Klein, *The complex quadric from the standpoint of Riemannian geometry*, doctoral thesis, University of Cologne, 2005, [open-access institutional version](https://kups.ub.uni-koeln.de/1627/1/Diss.pdf), §§1.1–1.2, §3.2, §3.4 and §8.1, on the quadric, its oriented-plane description and the low-dimensional models.

- Ravi Vakil, *Foundations of Algebraic Geometry*, Classes 15 and 16, 12 November 2007, updated 10 December 2007, [author-hosted notes](https://math.stanford.edu/~vakil/0708-216/216class1516.pdf), §5, especially Example 5.1, on the Segre map and the quadric surface.

The preceding lessons linked in the proofs supply the analytic, representation-theoretic and geometric foundations used here.
